tion that screenplays are not autonomous works has been most forcefully
made by Noél Carroll; the claim that screenplays are not complete works
has been made by a number of critics influenced by poststructuralist liter-
ary theory.°
This chapter addresses the first of these two objections—that is, the one
regarding the screenplay’s putative lack of autonomy—by reconstructing
and critiquing Carroll’s argument. To be fair to him, it is worth noting that
his remarks on the screenplay have all appeared as peripheral comments in
the context of broader discussions regarding the ontology of film. Never-
theless, he has maintained the same position in various publications over
the last 15 years—in his 1998 monograph, A Philosophy of Mass Art, and
updated in his 2006 essay, “Defining the Moving Image,” and his 2008
volume, The Philosophy of Motion Pictures—so | take him to be commit-
ted to it. In these publications, Carroll argues that there is a substantive
ontological difference between the theatrical script and the screenplay and,
furthermore, that the screenplay’s ontological status precludes it from being
a work of art.
I think this argument is worth reviewing at length both because it is the
only position on the screenplay on offer in Anglo-American aesthetics, and
because it is the clearest and most cogent formulation of a common claim
by film and literary theorists.” Carroll’s contributions to both aesthetics and
film studies have, of course, been enormous, and I am personally indebted
to him in a number of ways. However, I believe his view of the screenplay
is misguided. In the first half of this chapter, I reconstruct his argument and
contextualize it within his broader views on the ontology of multiple in-
stance artworks. In the second half of the chapter, I raise several objections.
Because I think the sort of argument Carroll presents ultimately misses the
mark due to a broader confusion of the relationships that obtain between
instructions for creating (or instancing) artworks and the works themselves,
my most robust counterargument takes the form of an alternative sketch
of these relationships, which comes in the following chapter. There, I con-
clude that screenplays are, in fact, the kinds of things that can be works of
literature. Finally, this conclusion provides a segue into Chapter 7, which
reconstructs and then rebuts the objection that screenplays are not the

Objections and OntologyI 113
kinds of things that can be works of literature because they are essentially
incomplete.
CARROLL’S ARGUMENT AGAINST SCREENPLAYS AS ARTWORKS
Carroll begins by claiming that we do not, in our standard appreciative
practices, treat screenplays the same way we treat theatrical scripts. Ac-
cording to Carroll, screenplays “are not read like plays and novels.” On
the contrary, he claims, “The only people who read screenplays, apart from
actors and directors, are people who are trying to learn how to write them
or the people who teach people to write them or film scholars engaged in
historical and/or critical research.” Furthermore, he reasons, “When scripts
are published that is typically because the films in question are thought to
be aesthetically important.”!? Notice here that Carroll does not advert to
these suppositions regarding our appreciative practices in order to argue
that screenplays are not artworks. That is, he is not making the argument—
which one supposes is open to him—that screenplays are not artworks
because we do not treat them as such. But he is suggesting that our appre-
clative practices indicate, prima facie, that screenplays are not artworks on
the order of theatrical scripts. Whether we should accept Carroll’s account
of our appreciative practices of screenplays is a question I shall address
later on. For now, it is enough to note that on his view, we just do not read
screenplays as we read theatrical scripts—that is, as works of literature.
Carroll then segues from a description of our practices to an explanation
of them by way of an example. According to him, “the voters at the Acad-
emy Awards do not read the scripts of nominees when they vote for the
best screenplay; they watch the movie in which it is a contributing creative
ingredient.”!' Here we see the beginning of the argument: the screenplay is
a kind of contributing ingredient in a film. More specifically, screenplays
are, in Carroll’s words, “constituents,” of films.'* This relationship between
the screenplay and the film is, in Carroll’s view, markedly different from the
relationship between the theatrical script and the theatrical performance.
Because Carroll draws a sharp contrast between the screenplay/film re-
lationship and the theatrical script/theatrical performance relationship,
we must look at his philosophy of theater—most fully elaborated in his
2006 essay “Philosophy and Drama”—quite closely.'? In his view, the art of
drama actually involves “two distinguishable art forms: the art of compos-
ing play texts (or, more broadly, performance plans) and the art of perform-
ing them.”'* Therefore, he writes, “Drama, in this respect, is a dual-tracked
or two-tiered art form.”!5 According to Carroll, drama as composition need
not take the form of a script (or, as he often calls it, “play text”); it could
be a performance plan that remains unwritten, in which case “the script,
so to speak . . . may live in the memory of the performers.”'* The script or
play text is, in fact, the form of drama as composition with which we are

114 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
most familiar, yet, Carroll suggests, all performance plans can be evaluated
in their own right, regardless of the form they take. For example, “an un-
transcribed harvest ceremony also has a performance plan, albeit unwritten,
that can be analyzed and appreciated independently of its performance, just
as a traditional folk dance has a design that can be scrutinized in isolation
from any particular performance of it.”!” In short, the suggestion is that all
performance plans are works of art inasmuch as they are products of “the
art of composing performance plans.”'®
However, Carroll suggests, at the same time theatrical scripts are prod-
ucts of “the art of composition,” they are also works of literary art.'” This is
a quick move that I shall look at more closely presently. The conclusion he
draws, though, is that, ontologically speaking, play texts are on the order of
other works of literature such as novels—abstract types, to which we have
access through token copies. That is, “your copy of Middlemarch and my
copy are both tokens of the type created by George Elliot. These tokens are
material objects that grant us access to the abstract type, the artwork Mid-
dlemarch.”»® So, Carroll thinks that such types are created and completed
by their authors. “Likewise,” he writes, “a drama as a literary work—The
Master Builder, for example—is fixed as the art type it is for all time in
terms of the relevant aesthetic elements that constitute it as determined by
the intentions of its author, Ibsen.”*!
However, Carroll points out, dramatic performances of the same play
texts obviously differ from one another. On his view, “This is because when
viewed from the perspective of drama as performing art, play texts are re-
garded simply as recipes—semiporous formulas to be filled in by executors
in the process of producing performance artworks—rather than as fixed
artworks in their own right.””* As recipes, Carroll writes, “play texts specify
the ingredients of the performance—such as lines of dialogue, characters,
and perhaps some props—as well as the range of global emotional tones or
flavors appropriate to the work.”*’ Or, to use another metaphor, he suggests,
“they are blueprints rather than finished buildings, figuratively speaking.”™4
The thought here is that the theatrical script necessarily underdetermines
performances of it. The consequences of this are that every performance of
a theatrical script necessarily requires an interpretation of it, and, therefore,
has an identity that is distinct from the script. Carroll explains, “In order
to be incarnated as a performance, every play text requires interpretative
activity on the parts of the executors who must extrapolate beyond what
has been written or otherwise previously stipulated (as in the case of orally
transmitted performance plans).”** It is actually this interpretive activity
that, on Carroll’s view, constitutes what he calls the art of performance.
Ontologically, then, an interpretation of a play text is a distinct artwork—
a distinct type. Carroll offers no specific example in his “Philosophy and
Drama” essay, but elsewhere he makes clear that he is thinking of produc-
tions as these interpretation types—for example, Katie Mitchell’s produc-
tion of Henry IV and Peter Sellars’s production of The Merchant of Venice.2®

Objections and OntologyI 115
“Thus,” he writes, “these interpretations of the recipe are themselves types
that then generate performance tokens.”2’ That is, individual performances
of Peter Sellars’s The Merchant of Venice are tokens of an interpretation
type, which is Sellars’s production.
Now, there is something odd going on here—to which I shall return—
because what Carroll calls “the art of performance” turns out to be the art
of interpretation. Individual performances amount not to distinct artworks,
but to tokens of an interpretation type, which is the artwork. It seems to
me that Carroll recognizes this problem and attempts to explain it away,
writing, “to enact that interpretation, to instantiate it tonight onstage re-
quires thought—requires an interpretation of the interpretation relevant to
the immediate circumstances of the live performance.”?® This proposition
raises a number of interesting questions that deserve further explanation:
for example, why is one interpretation a distinct artwork, but the other not?
But Carroll takes it to resolve matters and concludes, “drama is a two-tiered
or double-decker art form. There is drama as composition and drama as
performance.” Furthermore, Carroll writes, “we call an art a performing
art just in case it exhibits this duality.”°°
In Carroll’s The Philosophy of Motion Pictures, a condensed version of
this account of the ontology of theater lays the groundwork for his discus-
sion of the ontology of film. In one sense, his main point is, quite simply, that
film is not a performing art. Yet he attempts to get at this point by focusing
on this putative two-tiered nature of theater and the other performing arts:
[T]heater, like music and dance, can be called a two-tiered artform. In
music, at least since the advent of notation, there is the score—the art-
work created by the composer; and then there is also the performance—
the score as played by a group of musicians. Similarly, there is the
choreography of the dance which is one artwork; and then there is
choreography as performed by the troupe which constitutes a discrete
artwork. And in theater, there is the play—if not of the well-made sort
[i.e. in the form of a script] then, at least, some kind of performance
plan—and subsequently its execution (or executions).*!
However, Carroll stresses, “cinema is not a two-tiered artform. There is
one, and only one, artwork in cinema—the finished film or video.”** Again,
Carroll’s main goal here is to show that film is not a performing art. More
specifically, the point he wants to make is this: “the performance of a mo-
tion picture—a film showing—is not an artwork in its own right.”° Indeed,
typical film screenings are not performances properly so called—at least
on the order of theatrical or musical performances. Thus, Carroll argues
that cinema is not a two-tiered artform because “performances” of the film,
screenings, are not distinct works of art.
Yet the fact that argument is made in terms of “two-tieredness” has con-
sequences for the screenplay. That is, if film is not a two-tiered art form,

116 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
then it appears that the screenplay must be a part of a single work of art.
According to Carroll, “If, in theater, the play-text type is a recipe or sketch
that the director and/or the actors interpret, and, furthermore, if the recipe
and the interpretation can be regarded as different though related artworks,
in motion pictures the recipe and the interpretations are constituents of the
self-same integral artwork.”** That is, he elaborates, “In contrast to the
theater, where the recipe [i.e., the script] and the interpretation are two dif-
ferent artworks, in cinema the recipe and its interpretation are presented to-
gether in one indissoluble package.”** On this account, then, the difference
between theatrical scripts and screenplays seems to be that theatrical scripts
admit of multiple interpretations, which are performed, whereas screen-
plays are only used once in the production of a single film. In sum, Carroll
concludes, screenplays “are ontologically ingredients in the motion pictures
with which they are associated rather than being independent artworks.”*°
Call this the “ontological argument.”
The ontological argument is the core of Carroll’s argument that screen-
plays cannot be artworks. To it, he adds what I call the “transcription argu-
ment.” The gist of the transcription argument is this: Screenplays cannot be
artworks because they are not original compositions, but merely transcrip-
tions of films. In Carroll’s words, “The movie scripts that come to us in pub-
lished form are not the scripts used during the production process—which
were quite frequently being altered as the movie was being made—but are
more of the nature of transcriptions or records of the words that finally
would up being said in the finished product.”*’ If screenplays are mere tran-
scriptions of films, there is no reason to think that they are artworks in their
own right.
Carroll uses the transcription argument to bolster the ontological argu-
ment. That is, one of the reasons he thinks that screenplays are inseparable
from the films with which they are associated is that, qua transcriptions,
they depend upon the existence of those films for their own existence. As he
puts it, screenplays “are not the recipes that were used to launch an inter-
pretation. They are documents of what got made—less recipes, and more
parts of the dish that was served.”** According to this metaphor, then, the
screenplay is not—like a play script—a recipe, or set of instructions, that
is an artwork in its own right, but rather a constituent part of an artwork.
But here we see a tension in Carroll’s account of screenplays. First, he
argues, “in cinema the recipe and its interpretation are presented together
in one indissoluble package.”*? But then he argues that screenplays “are
not the recipes that were used to launch an interpretation.”*° Clearly, these
objections are mutually exclusive, but to be charitable I think we ought
to parse them like this: (1) If screenplays are mere transcriptions of films,
then they are not artworks (because a transcription is not an artwork);
(2) If screenplays are not transcriptions, but recipes—on the order of theat-
rical scripts—then they are not artworks because, unlike theatrical scripts,
they do not launch multiple interpretations. Furthermore, I think there is

Objections and Ontology I 117
third, implicit reason for why Carroll objects to the screenplay as artwork.
Because he has differentiated cinema from the performing arts by constru-
ing the latter as essentially two-tiered, he must maintain that cinema is not
a two-tiered art because it is obviously not a performing art. Thus, Car-
roll’s broader accounts of the ontology of the performing arts and of cinema
commit him to denying that screenplays can be artworks in their own right
alongside films themselves. I now turn to exploring these tensions in Car-
roll’s ontology of theater and ontology of film, and I rebut his arguments
against the screenplay as artwork.
OBJECTIONS TO CARROLL’S ARGUMENT
Before beginning to mount objections in earnest, we ought to first dis-
pute Carroll’s characterization of our appreciative practices with regard to
screenplays and claim that, on the contrary, we often do read screenplays
as we read plays or novels. I do not make a case for this claim at length,
but here are a few ideas to think about. When we browse in bookstores
these days, it is not uncommon to find more published screenplays in the
Film section than published theatrical scripts in the Drama section. There
are numerous publishers and imprints that now publish classic and con-
temporary screenplays on a regular basis. On the web, there are two major
collections of screenplays—simplyscripts.com and imsdb.com (The Internet
Movie Script Database). In addition, as I described in Chapters 1 and 2,
there are fan communities in which fans share both screenplays based on
previously existing television shows, movies, and characters, as well as
original screenplays. These fan screenplays are not intended to function as
production documents, but rather are created to be read for their own sake
by community members.
In short, I think that empirical investigation will reveal enough evidence
to suggest that screenplays are frequently read just as plays and novels
are read. And if this account of our appreciative practices is right, we can
add it to Chapter 4’s account of the creative practice of screenwriting as a
prima facie reason to think that screenplays are works of art in their own
right. Nevertheless, as I noted earlier, Carroll does not explicitly appeal
to our appreciative practices to support his argument, so let us leave this
matter aside and address his transcription argument and his ontological
argument.
The transcription argument is straightforwardly addressed by simply
conceding Carroll’s point. We should, I think, accept his argument that a
transcription of a film—published or otherwise—is not a work of art in its
own right. Such a transcription is less like a theatrical script than it is like a
video recording of the performance of a play. A transcription, like a video
recording of a performance, is just a record of an artwork on which it de-
pends for its existence and is not an artwork in its own right.

118 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
Nevertheless, I take it that screenplays are, by definition, not transcrip-
tions of films. Both inside and outside of the film industry, a screenplay is
roughly thought of as a document that is used to make a film—dare I say a
recipe or blueprint for the creation of a film—rather than a record of a film
that was made. Someone who calls a transcription of a film “a screenplay”
is simply misusing the term “screenplay.”
I grant that in the 1970s and 1980s there was some confusion about this
in the publishing industry. Publishers sometimes released objects that they
called “screenplays,” which were either screenplays that had been “cor-
rected” to conform to the theatrical print of the film or were simply tran-
scriptions of films. I suspect that part of the confusion stemmed from the
fact that Hollywood, during the Golden Age, used to distinguish between
something called a “continuity script,” which was like a final draft screen-
play used on set (roughly, what we now call a “shooting script”), and a “cut-
ting continuity script,” which was basically a transcript of the finished film
that could be sent to the Production Code Administration, state censors, or
exhibitors. In some cases, continuity scripts and cutting continuity scripts
are confused, or it is thought that a cutting continuity script is a screenplay
in the ordinary sense of the term. But this is simply an error. Publishing a
transcription of a film, whether it is a cutting continuity script or otherwise,
and calling it a screenplay is a mistake, for screenplays, properly so called,
are documents from which films are made. But, we should note, this is a
mistake that has largely been rectified by the publishing industry. Most of
the screenplays that are available now are published under imprints such as
Newmarket Press’s “The Newmarket Shooting Script Series,” and are, as
the name indicates, shooting scripts or writers’ final draft screenplays.
Even if the transcription argument depends on a simple misunderstand-
ing about what a screenplay is (and what it is not), defusing it is significant
because we have seen that it partially underpins Carroll’s core argument,
which is the ontological argument. One of the reasons Carroll insists that
screenplays are, ontologically, inseparable components of the films with
which they are associated is that screenplays are supposedly mere transcrip-
tions. I have argued that this is false and that the transcription argument
fails on this count. The ontological argument, then, is seriously weakened
insofar as screenplays, properly so called, are not ontologically inseparable
from films in the same way that transcriptions are.
But if the transcription argument fails, are screenplays not still insepa-
rable from the films with which they are associated in other ways such that
Carroll’s core argument succeeds? Note here that Carroll has set the bar
for success quite high. The ontological argument suggests that screenplays,
in virtue of the kinds of things they are, are inseparable from the films
with which they are associated. Recall that the argument is that screen-
plays “are ontologically ingredients in the motion pictures with which
they are associated rather than being independent artworks.”*! Because
this is an argument about the essential nature of cinema, it implies logical

Objections and Ontology I 119
necessity. This, in turn, means that a single counterexample will under-
mine the argument.
Is a screenplay logically inseparable from a film such that it could not
possibly be interpreted differently in the making of a different (but re-
lated) film? I think the answer has to be “no,” simply because we can easily
imagine our filmmaking practices being slightly different, such that it was
common for a single screenplay to be interpreted and shot on multiple occa-
sions in various different ways. But in any case, actual instances of this do
exist. Consider Gus Van Sant’s remake of Psycho (1998), which used a very
slightly modified version of Joseph Stefano’s screenplay for Hitchcock’s Psy-
cho (1960). Or consider the 1952 version of The Prisoner of Zenda, which
was shot from the same screenplay used to make the 1937 version.
In addition, I suspect that the widening accessibility of digital technology
may further change our practices, such that shooting a screenplay multiple
ways becomes a more common occurrence. Consider, for example, a 2007
contest sponsored by the so-called “first on-line social network for filmmak-
ing,” www.livemansion.com. Spike Lee served as the executive producer for
this project/contest called “LiveMansion: The Movie,” in which five finalists
made different short films with the same script and the same budget. Clearly
in this case, we cannot speak of the screenplay as an inseparable element of
a film or of, in Carroll’s terms, “the recipe and its interpretation . . . pre-
sented together in one indissoluble package.”*? And even if this conjecture
about the way in which digital technology might change screenwriting prac-
tices turns out to be wrong, the important point is that those practices could
change such that it might become standard practice to interpret the same
screenplay on multiple occasions; it is not a matter of logical necessity, but
of contingent fact that the screenplay is typically only interpreted once in the
production of a single film.
Furthermore, Carroll’s account of screenplays as non-detachable con-
stituent parts of films fails to account for the status of screenplays that are
never shot. Surely the fan-fiction screenplays I mentioned earlier do not fit
Carroll’s characterization. For these are screenplays that are not intended to
be associated with any actual film and never will be. This would not pose a
problem if Carroll were only offering a characterization of the screenplay,
but his claim is that screenplays are not works of art in their own right be-
cause of their ontological status—their essential nature.
In any case, Carroll does not provide any argumentation for his supposi-
tion that the putative fact that screenplays do not admit of multiple interpre-
tations entails that they are not works of art in their own right. Remember,
it is on these grounds that Carroll contrasts the screenplay with the theatri-
cal script. With the transcription argument, the claim is that “[screenplays]
are not the recipes used to launch an interpretation,” and with the ontologi-
cal argument, the claim is that, “In contrast to the theater, where the recipe
and the interpretation are two different artworks, in cinema the recipe and
its interpretation are presented in one indissoluble package.”** Now, so far

120 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
I have argued that most screenplays are, in fact, production plans or reci-
pes that require interpretations on the order of theatrical scripts. But why
should the fact that the screenplay is not interpreted on multiple occasions
preclude it from being an autonomous work of art, as long as it can be re-
garded and evaluated apart from its execution? Just as the theatrical script
underdetermines performances of it and requires an interpretive filling-in of
details, so, too, does the screenplay underdetermine the film made from it
and require an interpretation. Thus, the screenplay, like the theatrical script,
is subject to appreciation and evaluation independent of an interpretation of
it, even if there is only one interpretation. Indeed, theatrical scripts that are
only interpreted once in a single performance are no less works of art for it.
To put the question in broader terms, why should we think the fact that
a recipe launches multiple interpretations is necessary for it being an au-
tonomous work of art in the first place? In fact, there are good reasons to
think that no such necessary condition obtains. Think of architecture. As
with cinema, architecture is not a performing art, but relies upon a kind of
production plan, or recipe, for the creation of a (typically) single work. For
as with theatrical scripts, architectural plans necessarily underdetermine the
works made from them. Some filling-in of details is always required to get
from the plans to the work. (Whether we construe that filling-in of details
as an interpretive, let alone creative, activity is not relevant here.) Yet, archi-
tectural plans are frequently recognized as works of art in their own right.“
Consider, for example, some of Zaha Hadid’s drawings and paintings for
architectural works.** Such drawings and paintings are clearly works of art
in their own right, despite the fact that they are only interpreted once (if
at all) in the creation of an architectural work. So, admitting of multiple
interpretations cannot be a necessary condition for a recipe or plan to be an
autonomous work of art.
The example of architecture, I think, points to deeper problems with
Carroll’s notion of two-tiered arts and his account of the ontology of per-
forming arts. In the first place, his claim, “we call an art a performing art
just in case it exhibits this duality,” seems to conflate two-tiered arts with
performing arts. For it is clear that architecture is sometimes a two-tiered
art, inasmuch as it involves a plan and a building (which are sometimes
both works of art), yet it is not a performing art. However, this is not just
a quibble. Equating two-tiered arts with performing arts necessitates deny-
ing the autonomy of the screenplay as a distinct artwork precisely because
cinema is not a performing art.
However, I think that Carroll’s conception of the performing arts as two-
tiered depends on a rather contentious account of the ontology of those arts.
Let us return to his discussion of theater. Recall that drama is supposedly
a two-tier art because it comprises the art of composition and the art of
performance. Now, one particularly unsatisfying piece of Carroll’s exposi-
tion is, as I suggested previously, that the art of performance turns out to
actually be the art of interpretation. In Carroll’s view, “interpretations of

Objections and OntologyI 121
the recipe are themselves types that then generate performance tokens.”“¢ It
is important to understand that with the type/token model, the artwork is
the type, which, depending on one’s view, is created, discovered, or initiated
by the artist. So, on Carroll’s account, the creation of the second (after the
performance plan) artwork involves the activity of interpretation—not the
activity of performance. Now, I do not doubt that Carroll is right to say that
an artwork is created in virtue of this interpretation, but it is deeply counter-
intuitive to call this activity the art of performance. Furthermore, this view
seems to lead to a consequence that many would find odd and that I doubt
Carroll wants to accept: actual performances are tokens of artworks but are
not artworks in their own right.‘” Note, though, that Carroll must accept
this result in order to hold on to the claim that drama is a two-tiered art.
As I mentioned, I think Carroll is aware of these problems and attempts
to alleviate them by claiming that even though dramatic performances are
not artworks, they still involve interpretation. For example, he writes, “the
successful delivery of a token dramatic performance involves a token in-
terpretation of an interpretation type, and, inasmuch as that depends on
artistic understanding and judgment, it is a suitable object of aesthetic ap-
preciation.”*® However, this suggestion reveals a tension that gets at one of
the core problems with the notion of two-tiered arts. On this account, we
have two interpretations, each of which involves creativity and artistry, and
each of which is “a suitable object of aesthetic appreciation.” There is no
principled reason to think that both interpretations are not works of art.
There is, on the other hand, a good reason to think that even if an indi-
vidual performance is a token of an interpretation type, it is also a work of
art in its own right. For not only do performances appear to be interpreta-
tions on the order of interpretation types, but we also recognize that the
activity of dramatic performance—in and of itself and without regard for
whether it does any interpretive work—is an art.*” Moreover, conceiving of
the ontology of theater in this way allows us to say that two different per-
formances of a single interpretation—say Sean Mathias’s 2009 production
of Waiting for Godot starring Sir Ian McKellen on one night and his un-
derstudy the next night—simultaneously instance the same works (Beckett’s
theatrical work and Mathias’s interpretation), but are two different works
of art. Therefore, I submit, drama is, at least, a three-tiered artform, and I
suspect that this argument holds good for the other performing arts as well.
Yet, there is a second substantial tension in Carroll’s account of the on-
tology of theater and the other performing arts, which has more direct con-
sequences for thinking about the screenplay. What is especially odd about
Carroll’s suggestion, in The Philosophy of Motion Pictures, that launching
multiple interpretations is relevant to the art status of a performance plan,
is that he makes no such suggestion in his essay regarding the philosophy
of drama. That is, in the drama essay, he does not argue that theatrical
scripts are works of art because they admit of multiple interpretations. On
the contrary, he simply claims that, in the case of drama, there is an “art of

122 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
composition.” Intuitively, this seems right, but then Carroll goes further to
claim, the “play or performance plan can be discussed and evaluated in its
own right, that is, apart from its performance.”*°
Here, I think, is the nub of the problem: Carroll conflates “the play” and
“the performance plan.” The claim that there is an “art of composition,”
inasmuch as the playwright creates a play in virtue of composing a perfor-
mance plan, is unobjectionable. But this is not the same as claiming that the
performance plans are the works of art created through the art of composi-
tion. This latter claim suggests that performance plans, as a class of things,
are works of art in their own right. According to Carroll, the fact that the
theatrical script, as we know it, is a literary work is incidental to our regard-
ing it as an autonomous work. Its status as autonomous artwork owes to
the fact that it is part of that wider class of things, performance plans. But,
as I indicated earlier, this move is too quick. Even if we were to agree that
performance plans are works of art in their own right, then the theatrical
script is.an essentially different kind of artwork in virtue of its status as a
literary object. But is the theatrical script’s literary status really incidental
to the fact that it is an autonomous artwork? That is, is the theatrical script
actually a work of art in its own right because it is a performance plan and
irrespective of the fact that it is a literary work? More broadly, is being a
performance plan—a recipe that invites multiple interpretations—sufficient
for being an autonomous work of art?
The answer to these questions is “no,” and this is readily apparent when
we inspect Carroll’s attempts to expand his account to the other perform-
ing arts. In his view, it will be recalled, the performing arts are two-tiered
because they involve two distinct artworks—a performance plan and the
performance of it. Supposedly, these works are the musical score and musi-
cal performance, dance choreography and dance performance, and the the-
atrical performance plan and theatrical performance.*! Perhaps needless to
say, the difficulty here is that the performance plan of a musical work—the
score—is not a work of art. The musical score is just a set of instructions
that one must follow to instance a musical work. And, in our standard prac-
tices, it is the musical work, not the score, that we regard as art.*? So, one
point to note here is that if musical scores afford multiple interpretations
that result in distinct artworks, but are not themselves works of art, then af-
fording multiple interpretations cannot be sufficient for a performance plan
such as a theatrical script to be a work of art in its own right.
However, the larger point is that although the composer creates a musi-
cal work in virtue of completing a performances plan (the score), the plan
is not the work. Now a charitable reading of Carroll’s passage suggests that
he does not actually believe that scores are artworks in their own right, but
he is simply using the term “score” as a stand-in for “musical work.” I think
this is right, but it means that his notion of two-tiered arts owes to a distinc-
tion not between performance plan and performance, but between work
and performance, where the work is distinct from its performance plan.

Objections and Ontology I 123
The score is a work-determinative document**—not the work itself, nor a
work of art in its own right. Therefore, there is now no reason to think that
performance plans are necessarily works of art. Is the theater any different
from music in these regards? No. In the case of theater, the playwright cre-
ates a theatrical work in virtue of engaging in what Carroll calls “the art of
composition”—that is, composing a performance plan. So far so good. But
the theatrical work is not the performance plan. The theatrical work and
the performance plan are not to be conflated, for the same reason the musi-
cal work and the score are not to be conflated: Just as musical works are
things-to-be-heard, theatrical works are things-to-be-watched (and heard).
But if theatrical scripts are not works of art in virtue of the fact that they
are performance plans, then to what factors do they owe their art status?
I want to suggest that what makes the theatrical script a work of art in its
own right is, quite simply, the fact that it is a literary work. Moreover, I sub-
mit, its status as performance plan is entirely irrelevant to its art status. And
if this is right, we not only have reasons to reject Carroll’s argument that
screenplays are not artworks in their own right, but we also have reasons to
think that at least some screenplays can be autonomous artworks insofar as
they are, as are theatrical scripts, verbal objects—and, furthermore, special
kinds of literary works.
In order to mount these arguments, however, I must develop an alterna-
tive account of the ontology of the performing arts in order to clarify the
relationship between performance plan, work, and performance. In addi-
tion, I need to show how this account bears upon the relationship between
production plans and works in the non-performing arts of architecture and
cinema. These tasks are the focus of the next chapter.

6 Instructions and Artworks:
Musical Scores, Theatrical Scripts,
Architectural Plans, and Screenplays
What is the nature of the relationship between screenplay and film, and
how, if at all, does this relationship affect the screenplay’s art status? Hav-
ing critiqued, in the previous chapter, Noél Carroll’s account of that re-
lationship and his conclusion that screenplays are not the sorts of things
that can be art, the onus is now upon me to offer a positive account of
the screenplay/film relationship—one that meshes with my claim that many
screenplays are works of art in their own right. This is the goal of the pres-
ent chapter, the starting point of which is a pretheoretical intuition that the
screenplay is plausibly grouped with the musical score, the theatrical script,
and the architectural plan as a set of instructions for generating an artwork.'
If, as I shall argue, this intuition is roughly accurate, then analyzing the re-
lationship between screenplay and film in comparison to that which holds
between these other sets of instructions and their related artworks ought to
be a useful way of beginning to answer these questions.
Needless to say, however, there is a good deal of diversity among these
various kinds of instructions. Insofar as the screenplay is a verbal object that
typically relates a narrative to be enacted, its closest relative appears to be
the theatrical script. And for this reason, we may suspect that, like the the-
atrical script, the screenplay is oftentimes a work of dramatic literature. Yet
the relationship between screenplay and film is significantly different from
that between theatrical script and theatrical work. Unlike theater, cinema is
not a performing art, and, unlike the theatrical script (and musical score),
the screenplay is not a plan for instancing a work for performance. Rather,
it is a set of instructions for one step (i.e., shooting) in the creation of a work
that is not for performance. In this regard, the screenplay appears to be
more similar to an architectural plan than to a theatrical script. For we do
not think of an architectural plan as instructions for generating an instance
of a work for performance, but, again, as instructions for the creation of a
work that is not for performance.
It is also worth asking what bearing the relationship between instructions
and artwork has upon the art status of the instructions themselves—not
only in the case of the screenplay, but more generally. For although musical
scores and theatrical scripts are both sets of instructions for the instancing of
performance works, the former are almost never artworks in their own right

Instructions and Artworks 125
and the latter almost always are. On the other hand, architectural plans
seem sometimes to be works of art in their own right and sometimes not.
A clarification of the nature of the relationships that hold between these
various sets of instructions and their related artworks, as well as the princi-
ples by which the art status of (some of) those instructions is secured, would
be helpful here. To some extent, this has already been done by philosophers
working on the ontology of music, theatre, and architecture.2 However, the
work has largely proceeded in piecemeal fashion.’ Therefore, I think it is
worthwhile to systematically detail the relationships between instructions
and artwork for each of these art forms—in part because it is valuable in
its own right, but more importantly in the present context because it can
help us answer overlooked fundamental questions about the nature of the
screenplay and its relationship to the film.
I begin by sketching the relationship between musical score and musi-
cal work, as well as between theatrical script and theatrical work. I argue
that there is a fundamental similarity in these relationships. Both scores and
scripts are work-determinative.* In completing a musical score or theatrical
script, the composer or playwright thereby creates a musical or theatrical
work, which may be instanced on multiple occasions by following the in-
structions in the score or script.° Despite this similarity, musical scores are
typically not works of art in their own right, while theatrical scripts are. I
conclude that this difference owes simply to the fact that the theatrical script
is a verbal object and, thus, a potential work of dramatic literature indepen-
dent of the fact that it is work-determinative.
I then contrast these work-determinative documents with architectural
plans. I argue that no work of architecture is created in virtue of the comple-
tion of an architectural plan, because the work of architecture has some
constitutive properties that depend upon the physical materials with which
it is built and the spatio-temporal location in which it is built. Likewise,
although the screenplay is a set of instructions for the creation of a film,
it is not work-determinative, because a film is not created in virtue of the
completion of a screenplay. For a cinematic work has some constitutive
properties that depend upon the production and postproduction processes.
However, I conclude, there is no reason to think that this fact in any way
precludes the screenplay from being a work of art in its own right. On the
contrary, the screenplay’s status as verbal object gives it the potential to be
a work of literary art in the same way the theatrical script’s status as ver-
bal object makes it a potential work of literary art. That is, like theatrical
scripts, screenplays can be special kinds of literary works.
MUSICAL SCORES
In The Screenplay: Authorship, Theory and Criticism, Steven Price writes,
“the differences between a verbal text and musical notation, between the
readerships of a screenplay and a musical score . . . are almost too numerous

126 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
to be worth unpacking.”® This dismissal strikes me as a missed opportunity,
because reflecting upon the nature of the musical score and its relationship
to the musical work can actually offer us a great deal of insight about the
nature of the screenplay. In this passage, Price is discussing a juxtaposition
of score and screenplay made in two different contexts by Peter Wollen
and Douglas Garrett Winston, respectively. Winston writes, “Just as no one
would claim that reading an orchestral score is as satisfying as hearing it
performed, equally no one would claim that a script or synopsis is an ad-
equate substitute for a completed motion picture.”’ What is interesting to
me here is the category mistake Winston supposes someone could make in
attempting to enjoy a musical work by reading the score. Of course, there is
no sense in which reading a score could be as “satisfying” as hearing it per-
formed, because the score neither is the musical work, nor does it instance
the work.
The thought of someone reading a score in an attempt to access a musi-
cal work may indeed seem odd. But, I think, it is understandable enough
that one might, at first glance, mistakenly think that the score instances the
work, or think that the work can be identified with the score. For exam-
ple, the fact that Beethoven read and composed scores, apparently able to
“hear” the works in his head, may seem to indicate that the score instances
the work. One might go further and claim the fact that Bruckner composed
his Nullified Symphony—which was not performed until after his death—in
virtue of writing a score indicates that the score is the work. That is, because
it seems clear enough that the composer creates a musical work by writing
the score, it may appear that the work is to be identified with the score.
In order to see the problems with these two views, let us briefly consider
what kind of a thing a musical work is. A cursory glance at contemporary
debates reveals a striking variety of theories of the nature of musical works
on offer. However, with a few notable exceptions, most philosophers en-
dorse some variety of realism, regarding musical works as abstract objects—
usually types of some sort that are tokened through performances. But for
type theorists of all stripes and even most philosophers who do not promote
type theories, musical works are nevertheless either instanced or constituted
by performances.‘
It seems uncontroversial, then, to say that our appreciation of musi-
cal works—whatever they are—depends necessarily upon performances of
them insofar as musical works are “things-to-be-listened-to.” But if this
is right, then the score is not to be identified as the musical work for sev-
eral reasons: first, scores are not “things-to-be-listened-to,” and second,
as Roman Ingarden writes, “The work possesses characteristics that do
not pertain to the score and vice versa.”? In his recent book, Julian Dodd
identifies these challenges to the score-as-work view and adds two more:
“First... some pieces do not have a score .. . Second, works of music can
survive the destruction of their original scores.”!° So, the work is not to be
identified with the score.

Instructions and Artworks 127
Furthermore, however, it cannot be the case that the score instances the
work. For, again, inasmuch as a musical work is “a ‘thing-to-be-listened-to”
instances of it also have properties—tonal qualities, rhythm, and duration—
that the score does not. This is not to deny that a skilled sight-reader
may be able to appreciate some properties—say, for example, structural
properties—of musical works by reading the score alone.!! However, it does
not follow that by reading the score one perceives an instance of the work.
In sum, we can say that although a musical score supplies the instructions
for the instancing of a musical work, the work itself can only be instanced
through a performance.
But if a score is neither a musical work, nor an instance of one, then
what is it? Insofar as the score grounds the facts about what a musical
work is like, and insofar as correctly executing the instructions it specifies
will generate an instance of the work, the score is a work-determinative
document. “The score thus designates what the work is to be like,” as In-
garden writes.'* To put it somewhat more precisely, I suggest we think of
the score as Stephen Davies describes it: “a score is a musical notation the
main purpose of which is to serve as a work prescription. It records a set of
instructions, addressed to performers, the faithful execution of which gen-
erates an instance of the piece it specifies.”'* Note that the score-as-work-
determinative view recognizes that although the score determines the iden-
tity of the work, it nevertheless underdetermines performances of the work.
In other words, because a score never specifies all of the constitutive ele-
ments of a performance, every performance of a scored work requires an in-
terpretation of the score. As Davies writes, “There is no instance of the work
without a performance and no performance without an interpretation.”' *
However, although a performance instances the musical work by inter-
preting the score, it does not instance the score itself. For the score itself
can no more be instanced by a performance of a musical work than it can
instance a musical work. The score is just a set of written notations—an
ordering of various symbols constituted by lines and dots—that in no way
can be instanced by playing musical instruments. It is for this reason that
scores are not works of art in their own right: As a set of written notations,
the score is not a musical work. And in our standard practices, there is no
art of creating musical notations.
So if scores—as a class of things—were to be art, they would have to be
some other kind of artworks (unless our creative and appreciative practices
changed such that an “art of the score” came to be). But it is not clear what
kind of a work that could be. This is not to deny that some scores do have
appreciable artistic or aesthetic qualities. In fact, it is plausible that a given
score can be a work of art if it is presented with the right sort of intentions
or, more simply, if it is created in a recognizable art form such as drawing—
as in the case of Cornelius Cardew’s graphic score, Treatise. However, if
graphic scores such as this are indeed works of art, it is because they are
drawings—not because they are scores. Aside from such special cases, scores

128 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
do not appear to be artworks. They are valuable objects because of their
work-determinative relationship to works of art—not because they them-
selves are works of art.
THEATRICAL SCRIPTS
The fact that musical scores are not works of art is the crucial difference be-
tween them and theatrical scripts. For in most other ways, theatrical scripts
are quite like musical scores. Unfortunately, this single, peculiar quality of
the theatrical script can cause a great deal of confusion about the ontology
of theater. Recall the quotation from Winston describing the category mis-
take made by someone who attempts to appreciate a symphony by reading
the score or enjoy a film by reading the screenplay. I worry that there is no
mention of theater here precisely because there is a common misperception
that reading a theatrical script does, in fact, give one access to a theatrical
work.
However, a theatrical work cannot be identified with a script for the same
reasons we saw that a musical work cannot be identified with its score. For
one, not all theatrical works have scripts. Most “happenings,” for example,
are dramatic works that are not linked to any scripts. Second, it is possible
for theatrical works to survive the destruction of their scripts. As long as
some theater company or troupe can stage a correct performance of any
given theatrical work, then the work exists.
Finally, and perhaps most importantly, the theatrical work is not to be
conflated with the script, because just as musical works require perfor-
mances for their instantiation since they are essentially things-to-be-heard,
theatrical works require performances for their instantiation since they are
essentially things-to-be-watched (and nearly always heard, as well). Indeed,
one might go even further and accept Paul Woodruff’s recent argument,
“The purpose of theater, stated simply, is watching.”! 5B ut whether one ac-
cepts Woodruff’s definition of theater is not as important as recognizing the
validity of his more modest conclusion: “Literature has no necessary part in
the definition of theater, which does not require a text.”'® Another philoso-
pher of theater, James R. Hamilton, writes, “there simply is no theatrical
mode of presentation of works of dramatic literature.”'” I think Hamilton
is right about this, but want to resist his further, stronger claim: “a perfor-
mance is, accordingly, never a performance of some other work.”!’ By my
account, a theatrical performance may be of a theatrical work that exists
prior to the performance. However, it needs to be clear that this theatrical
work is not to be identified with the script. As Nicholas Wolterstorff puts it:
A copy of the script for a drama is not a copy of the drama but instruc-
tions for proper performances thereof. The script may of course be a
literary work in its own right. And that work can have both readings

Instructions and Artworks 129
aloud and copies. But the drama is not the script. And a copy of the
script 1s not a copy of the drama. The drama has no copies. All it has
are performances. Dramas are only performance-works.'?
In sum, neither is a theatrical work ever a script, nor is it ever instanced by
a script. On the other hand, if a script is a work of art, it is not a theatrical
work,
As I suggested previously, the idea that scores and scripts cannot be
identified as musical works or theatrical works (or instances of them) may
initially strike one as counterintuitive because we want to allow that com-
posers and playwrights create works even if they are never performed. For
example, in 1947 Beckett wrote a play called Eleutheria. The play was not
performed until 2005, but Beckett had created a theatrical work long before
then. Indeed, we would still say that Beckett created a theatrical work called
Eleutheria even if it was never performed. But here we must be careful not
to confuse the script with the theatrical work. For the theatrical work is not
something to be read, but something to be performed and watched and, as
such, has properties that a verbal object like a script does not.
The fact that the playwright creates a theatrical work in virtue of writing
the script—but which cannot be identified with the script—becomes clearer
if we consider that some scripts are not necessarily verbal objects. Consider
visual scripts—such as Dick Higgins’s Graphis 82—which drama theorist
David Cole characterizes as “non-representational graphic pattern[s]” in-
tended to be performed.” Visual scripts should remind us that despite the
potential confusion caused by the fact that theatrical scripts are usually ver-
bal objects, they are, nevertheless, not theatrical works. Just as in the case of
music, the composer creates a musical work in virtue of composing a score
that is itself neither the work itself nor an instance of it, so, too, in theater,
the playwright creates a theatrical work in virtue of writing a script that is
itself neither the work nor an instance of it.
The visual script example also shows that a performance of a theatrical
work cannot be said to instance its script. Perhaps this seems counterintui-
tive, as well. After all, insofar as the script usually contains dialogue that
is spoken in the performance of the work, don’t we want to say that the
performance instances the script? Again, we must remember that scripts and
performances are two different kinds of things. Theatrical scripts are usually
purely verbal objects that contain dialogue and verbal descriptions of how
the dialogue should be spoken, how the characters are to behave, what the
lighting, sound design, and scenery are like, and so forth. Of these elements,
a theatrical performance can only instance dialogue. For the performance
does not instance verbal descriptions of dialogue delivery, character behav-
ior, lighting, sound, scenery and so forth, but rather involves those actual
things.?! And, of course, a theatrical performance cannot instance a visual
script comprising a series of lines or other graphics. Thus, we ought to con-
clude with Roman Ingarden, “it would be a mistake to claim that the stage

130 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
play is . . . a realization of a corresponding purely literary work... In the
stage play we are. . . dealing with a different type of work than the purely
literary work. In spite of that, there is a close connection.””? What, then, is
the nature of that connection between the theatrical script and the theatrical
work?
Like musical scores, theatrical scripts are work-determinative. That is,
even though theatrical scripts are neither theatrical works nor their in-
stances, they determine the identities of theatrical works. In virtue of writing
a script, Beckett created a theatrical work called Eleutheria. For by complet-
ing the script, Beckett fixed the constitutive properties of the theatrical work
and, thus, the work’s identity. Therefore, correctly following Beckett’s script
to mount a performance will result in an instance of the theatrical work.
Furthermore, in the same way that new instances of Beethoven’s Fifth Sym-
phony can be generated from a score, Beckett’s script can be used over and
over again to generate new instances of the theatrical work.
Yet, despite the fact that a theatrical work is created in virtue of writing a
script, there is no instance of the work without a performance—just as there
is no instance of a musical work without a performance. And like the musi-
cal score, the theatrical script underdetermines performances. Therefore, a
theatrical performance requires an interpretation of the script in the same
way a musical performance requires an interpretation of the score. How-
ever, although the performance of a theatrical work involves an interpreta-
tion of the script, the performance does not instance the script. For, as we
have seen, the script is necessarily distinct from the theatrical work.
Nevertheless, theatrical scripts are works of art. Why? We have seen that
it cannot be because they are theatrical works or instances of theatrical
works, for they are not. Furthermore, it cannot be because they can be in-
terpreted differently and on multiple occasions in the creation of perfor-
mances of theatrical works. Sherri Irvin suggests, “What is often of interest,
in assessing a work of drama, is the nature of the possibilities it creates for
performance.”* This seems right, but note that such interest cannot secure
the art status of theatrical scripts (nor does Irvin claim it can). For musical
scores also invite multiple interpretations and are involved in multiple per-
formances of musical works, but musical scores are not artworks. By my ac-
count, then, theatrical scripts must be distinct artworks for another reason.
That reason, I think, is simple enough: Theatrical scripts, in most cases,
are special kinds of literary works of art—works of dramatic literature.
Once we acknowledge the fact that the script does not instance the theat-
rical work, but is only work-determinative, it becomes apparent that the
script can only be a work of art insofar as it is some other kind of work.
Because scripts are typically verbal objects, it seems clear that they are can-
didates for counting as literary works. Perhaps some visual scripts are works
of art, inasmuch as they are drawings or paintings. But if standard scripts—
constituted entirely by words—are works of art, it seems that they must be
literary works, for there does not appear to be any other sort of work such

Instructions and Artworks 131
scripts could be said to be or to instance. Furthermore, insofar as most the-
atrical scripts can meet whatever descriptive criteria one accepts for count-
ing as literature, regarding them as such should not be controversial. So
theatrical scripts are two things: theatrical work-determinative documents
and dramatic literary works.
Conceiving of theatrical scripts as having this dual status resolves the
apparent paradox we face by noting that theatrical scripts are works of art
in their own right, yet do not actually instance theatrical works. When we
read a theatrical script as an autonomous work of art, we engage not with a
theatrical work, but rather with a literary work. For even though the script
does not furnish us with an instance of the theatrical work, it is itself an
instance of a distinct and different work. This work, to which we do have
direct access, is purely verbal in nature. Thus, it may be a work of literature
as long as it meets whatever conditions one accepts for being literature; the
fact that it is a work-determinative document is incidental to its art status.
ARCHITECTURAL PLANS
An architectural plan cannot be identified as the architectural work itself
or an instance of it for the same reason neither musical score nor theat-
rical script can be identified with musical work or theatrical work or an
instance thereof. Architectural works have properties that their plans do
not. A building must be erected for us to access an architectural work—not
unlike the way that a musical score must be performed for us to access the
musical work and a theatrical script must be performed for us to access the
dramatic work.
However, there is a crucial way in which architectural plans differ from
scores and scripts. Unlike the musician and playwright, who create abstract
works by finishing their instructions, the architect does not create an archi-
tectural work in virtue of completing a set of architectural plans. Architec-
tural works, as we commonly think of them, are not works for performance,
but rather physical objects, and they have constitutive properties that de-
pend upon the materials with which they are built as well as the sites upon
which they are built.
This claim, I recognize, raises some complex ontological questions that
I cannot address here. In his brief essay “Notations,” Stephen Davies de-
scribes architectural plans (as well as screenplays) as “difficult cases” be-
cause, depending on how we conceive of the ontology of an architectural
work, one may want to say—contra the position sketched above—that ar-
chitectural plans are work-determinative and that the completion of the plan
constitutes the completion of an architectural work.** The appeal of such
a position comes, I think, from considering cases of non-Western architec-
ture, which may be ontologically different from Western architecture. For
example, Dominic McIver Lopes has argued that some works of Japanese

132 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
architecture are not material objects.?> But if works of architecture are not
necessarily material objects, perhaps it is plausible that architectural plans
may be work-determinative. To sidestep some of the ontological questions
that are beyond my purview here, let me limit my immediate focus to West-
ern architecture.
The notion that architectural works must be material objects is sup-
ported by how we value them. Consider, for example, The Guggenheim
Museum designed by Frank Lloyd Wright. Wright’s architectural work has
survived a 1992 interior restoration and addition, as well as a 2005-2008
external restoration. So, works of architecture can survive some changes in
the materials that constitute them. But now supposed the entire building
is leveled. And let us further suppose that Wright’s plans for the building
are extant, whether or not they actually are. My hunch is that we would
feel that the leveling of the Guggenheim constituted the destruction of the
work—indeed that the work was lost to us. Moreover, we would not accept
that using Wright’s plans to “rebuild the Guggenheim” would re-instance
the same architectural work, but would create a distinct (albeit closely re-
lated) work.
The difficulty of “rebuilding” the Guggenheim is that the identity of a
work of architecture is partially constituted by the materials used to make
it, but is also partially a matter of where and when it is built. That is, ar-
chitectural works have constitutive properties that depend on the materials
with which they are constructed, but also upon their spatio-temporal loca-
tions. For example, the Burj Al Arab, which is designed to look like a ship’s
sail, has constitutive properties in virtue of the fact that it is surrounded by
water that it could not have had until it was actually constructed upon a
small island. Therefore, it seems the hotel did not exist as the architectural
work with which we are familiar until it was built.2° Furthermore, because
the Burj Al Arab has some of its constitutive properties in virtue of where,
when, and with what it was built, but it cannot be the case that using the
original architectural plans to construct another edifice will necessarily re-
sult in an additional instance of the same architectural work.
In short, we have good reasons to think that architectural works must be
built in order to be created and that multiple buildings constructed from the
same plan are not necessarily instances of the same work. Now there is a
positive argument to be made that multiple buildings constructed from the
same plan can be instances of the same work, but I want to eschew the ques-
tion of whether architecture is sometimes a multiple-instance art.”
Instead, the points I would like to draw out here are these: First, ar-
chitectural works differ from musical and theatrical works insofar as their
existence and their identities depend upon their instantiation (i.e., with what
materials, in what geographical location, in what socio-historical context
they are built). This is not true of scored musical works and scripted the-
atrical works, which are created and whose identities are fixed before they
are ever instanced.** Second, correctly using the same architectural plan to

Instructions and Artworks 133
create multiple buildings will not necessarily result in multiple instances of
the same architectural work. Even if we accept that multiple instances of
the same work may result—perhaps if the buildings are constructed by the
same people, with the same materials, on the same site, at roughly the same
time—it is hardly necessary that the same work would be instantiated. For
the specific conditions under which the building is erected will affect its con-
stitutive properties. On the other hand, correctly following the instructions
specified in a musical scores or a theatrical script will generate an instance of
the same work every time. Therefore, it seems clear that architectural plans
are unlike musical scores and theatrical scripts in this important regard:
Musical scores and theatrical scripts are work-determinative, but architec-
tural plans are not.
But then what is the relationship between architectural plans and archi-
tectural works? Simply put, I think we want to characterize architectural
plans more modestly, as instructions for the creation of architectural works.
It is true that an architectural plan very often has an intimate relationship
with a particular work of architecture. In many cases, a set of plans will
be created for the sole purpose of creating a single work. Nevertheless, the
identity of this particular work of architecture is not solely determined by
the set of plans, and following the plans to construct another building on
another occasion will not necessarily generate another instance of the same
work. Thus, architectural plans and architectural works are not ontologi-
cally tethered in the way musical scores and theatrical scripts are to musical
works and theatrical works, respectively.
Notice here that the preceding discussion of the nature of architectural
plans and their relationship to architectural works has nothing whatsoever
to do with their art status. Understanding why is straightforward. As with
musical scores and theatrical scripts, art status does not depend on the re-
lationship between the set of instructions and the work it is used to create.
In the case of musical scores and theatrical scripts, we saw that being work-
determinative does not affect whether the set of instructions can be a work
of art in its own right. Similarly, the fact that architectural plans are not
work-determinative, but are nevertheless involved in the creation of archi-
tectural works, does not in any way determine whether or not they are
works of art in their own right. On the contrary, all that matters is that they
meet the criteria that any candidate artwork must meet.
This account explains why some architectural plans—such as blueprints—
are not artworks, while some architectural plans—such as Zaha Hadid’s
Cardiff Bay Opera House plans—are. The difference amounts to the fact
that Hadid’s plans are paintings, and, as such, are uncontroversially art-
works. The only possible source of confusion is that Hadid is commonly
recognized not just as a visual artist, broadly speaking, but also as an archi-
tect. Yet I do not think that this is an insurmountable challenge. It seems fair
to say that if Hadid is an architect, properly so called, it must be because
she has created plans that could be used to create a work of architecture.

134 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
If her plans could not be used to create a work of architecture, then she is
only an artist insofar as she draws or paints images of buildings.”
In any case, we can see that nothing about the relationship between an
architectural plan and its use in the creation of an architectural work bears
upon whether the plan is a work of art in its own right. The plans for archi-
tectural works that are never created can be works of art, just as theatrical
scripts are works of art even if they are never used to stage a performance
of a theatrical work. On the other hand, using those plans to create an
architectural work does not in any way change the art status of the plans
themselves. Their art status is secured solely by virtue of the way it is for any
other candidate work that does not happen to be involved in the creation
of a distinct work.
SCREENPLAYS
So, what kind of a set of instructions is a screenplay? In terms of its re-
lationship to the finished film, it is—perhaps surprisingly—more like an
architectural plan than a theatrical script.*° Indeed, much of the preceding
discussion of the relationship between architectural plans and architectural
works can be applied mutatis mutandis to the relationship between screen-
plays and films. For, like architectural plans, screenplays are not work-
determinative.
Stephen Davies also notes a similarity in relationships between architec-
tural plan/architectural work and screenplay/ film. However, he suggests
that, like architectural plans, screenplays raise difficult questions. In both
cases, one may wonder whether the instructions do, in fact, specify works.
Davies writes, “[I|f we think of a remake based on the same script as a fur-
ther instance of the same work, the script looks to be work-specifying.”*!
The alternative view, which I take, “holds that the remake is a different
but related movie, even when based on the same script, in which case the
script is best thought of as a sketch or design for the work, but not as
work-specifying.”
On my account, screenplays are not work specifying because, according
to our present creative and appreciative practices, no cinematic work is cre-
ated in virtue of the completion of a screenplay. Films, as we know them,
have constitutive properties that are not determined until much later in the
production process—during shooting, editing, and sound mixing. For ex-
ample, a constitutive property of Battleship Potemkin (1925) is Eisenstein’s
innovative montage editing. A film that was shot from the same screenplay
but which did not have this property would not be a different instance of
Battleship Potemkin, but a very different film altogether. Indeed, it is largely
in virtue of this property that we value the film. I teach undergraduates
that Battleship Potemkin is an important work in the history of cinema be-
cause of its novel approach to editing. Without this property, we would have

Instructions and Artworks 135
a different work of less value and historical importance. So, a screenplay
alone does not make a cinematic work.
Thus, inasmuch as some of the constitutive properties of a film are deter-
mined after the completion of the screenplay, remakes made from the same
screenplay are not instances of the same cinematic work, but are distinct
works. Because scores and scripts are work-determinative, correctly follow-
ing the instructions they specify will necessarily instance the same musical or
theatrical work every time. However, re-using a single screenplay—like re-
using a single architectural plan—will not necessarily (if ever) generate new
instances of the same work. For re-using Eisenstein’s screenplay for Battle-
ship Potemkin will not result in a film that has the property of being innova-
tively edited as part of Eisenstein’s theoretical experiments with montage—a
property that our appreciative practices indicate is constitutive of the work,
Battleship Potemkin. Indeed, if all the copies of the film were destroyed, we
would be dismayed because the work was lost. Knowing that the screenplay
survived would not assuage our grief because following the instructions
therein will not supply us with an instance of the lost work. On the contrary,
instances of cinematic works are encoded by templates such as film prints
and DVDs. So, screenplays are not work-determinative.
It is also worth noting that when two films are made from the same
screenplay, they do not instance that screenplay. No film instances a screen-
play for the same reason that no theatrical performance instances a theatri-
cal script. Simply put, there is much more to both kinds of manuscripts than
dialogue. In the case of theater, we saw that performances do not instance
scripts because whereas scripts contain verbal descriptions of what settings,
costumes, props, lighting, and sounds are to be like, performances involve
those very things. So, too, it is in the case of film. Films do not instance
verbal descriptions of character behavior, settings, lighting, camera move-
ments, and so forth, but actually comprise those things. In this sense, it is
misleading to speak of a film—a remake or otherwise—as instancing or
“realizing” a screenplay.
Finally, what about the art status of the screenplay? Is a screenplay the
kind of thing that can be a work of art, and, if so, what kind of a work of
art? The short answers are “yes” and “a special kind of literary work.” Let’s
take each question in turn. First, we have seen in the cases of music and
theater that if a work-determinative document such as a script or score is a
work of art in its own right, it is only so because it also has an existence as
a work that can be intended and regarded as a complete, autonomous work
apart from its status as work-determinative document. Thus, the status of
Beckett’s script Eleutheria as a work of literature is unrelated to the fact that
the script is work-determinative of the theatrical work Eleutheria. Think
of Beethoven’s Fifth Symphony: We do not treat the score as an artwork in
its own right because it specifies a musical work. On the contrary, for such
4 work-determinative document to be a work of art, it must be the sort of
thing that can be intended and regarded as an artwork in its own right.

136 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
Musical scores, because they are written in musical notation, are not these
sorts of things. However, theatrical scripts can be, if only because they are
written in language.
Screenplays, it turns out, are not work-determinative, but neither does
the fact that a set of instructions is not work-determinative affect the in-
structions’ art status. Architectural plans are easily identified as works of art
when they are composed in some recognizable art form such as drawing or
painting. The relationship between the architectural plan and the work it is
used to create has no bearing on whether a plan is also a work of art in its
own right. So, too, it is with screenplays. If anything, the fact that screen-
plays are not work-determinative ought to make it even clearer that some
can be works of art in their own right. This is because sets of instructions
such as architectural plans and screenplays are not ontologically tethered
to any particular work in the way that scores and scripts are. An extended
analysis was required to explain how it is that theatrical scripts are simul-
taneously work-determinative and different works of art in their own right.
Because screenplays are not work-determinative, it should be comparatively
easy to grasp the idea that a screenplay is logically distinct from any particu-
lar film and, thus, is potentially a different work of art.
The bottom line is that, as a verbal object, a screenplay is a kind of liter-
ary work just in case it meets whatever criteria one normally applies for a
verbal object to count as a literary work. Its relationship to the film is ir-
relevant in this regard, just as the relationships between musical score and
musical work, theatrical script and theatrical work, architectural plan and
architectural work are all irrelevant in determining the art status of those
sets of instructions. All that remains, then, is to establish that some screen-
plays are literary works in generally the same way as are theatrical scripts by
successfully identifying screenplays that meet the sorts of criteria commonly
used in identifying any other work of literature.** I shall turn to this task
in the next chapter, but first I want to briefly address a couple of potential
objections to the arguments I have offered here.
OBJECTIONS
The arguments I have made in this final section on screenplays are those that
I believe will be most unfamiliar to readers and that I have the most interest
in defending. Therefore, I will limit my focus here to objections pertaining
to my comments on screenplays.
One may wonder if it could not be possible for screenplays to be work-
determinative in the way that theatrical scripts are.** Suppose Beckett con-
ceived of his screenplay for Film in just the same way as any of his theatrical
scripts: Film, on this view, is intended to be made on multiple occasions
with different actors, and so forth. Why not say that in virtue of writing the
screenplay, Beckett has created a cinematic work waiting to be instanced?

Instructions and Artworks 137
I think we should say he has not for the reason rehearsed above: as we know
them, cinematic works have constitutive properties that depend upon how
they are shot, edited, and so forth.
However, it seems to me that this is a contingent fact rather than a neces-
sity. I imagine our cinematic practices could change such that we conceived
of cinematic works differently—perhaps as somewhat ontologically similar
to musical works or theatrical works, despite the fact that those works are
for performance. Why couldn’t it become standard practice to shoot a single
screenplay on many occasions, regarding the results as instantiations of a
cinematic work specified by the screenplay? At the least, what would seem
to be required is that screenplays would have to be “left alone” after their
completion such that the instructions therein were understood as work-
prescriptions, and we would need some tacit collective agreement about
what sorts of features screenplays could prescribe for cinematic works. Such
a situation may be unlikely, but it seems possible.
David Davies agrees with me that the screenplay is not work-determinative.
For a screenplay is not, like a theatrical script, “an ur-work,” that prescribes
the features necessary for something to be an instance of a work. Thus, Da-
vies also agrees a screenplay can be an autonomous work of literature—not
because screenplays, as a class of things are literature, but because any given
screenplay might meet whatever conditions are sufficient for something to
be literature.*°
However, according to Davies, the prescriptive function that work-
determinative objects such as theatrical scripts and musical scores have (and
screenplays do not) is an important part of our appreciation of so-called
“ur-works.” Part of our interest in work-determinative documents is what
they make possible for work-instances. Screenplays, Davies suggests, do not
offer us this kind of interest. I think Davies is right about this, but would
