of the identity conditions of screenplays, so that we know when we are
dealing with a single screenplay and when we are dealing with multiple
screenplays.

164 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
To ask about the identity conditions or, relatedly, existence conditions of
the screenplay is to engage in a fundamental inquiry about its ontology. In
this view, an ontological investigation of the screenplay asks a very broad
question—What kind of a thing is a screenplay?—but it attempts to answer
this by asking some very specific questions about identity and existence:
What are the conditions under which screenplays are one and the same?
What are the conditions under which a screenplay exists (including when it
is created and destroyed)? Given other recent work on the ontology of the
screenplay, these questions might strike some theorists as rather prosaic.’
However, as I hope the preceding paragraphs have shown, these rather ab-
stract matters have important implications for how we answer the practical
questions raised by our ordinary dealings with screenplays.
Thus, this chapter begins to sketch an account of the ontology of the
screenplay, albeit one that is limited in scope. I leave the broad ques-
tion of what kind of a thing it is unanswered and, although I suggest a
rough framework for thinking about what its identity conditions could be,
I proposal no specific set of conditions. Because work on the ontology of
the screenplay is just beginning and because it is evident that theorists are
working with very different sets of methodological assumptions, my focus
is primarily on the antecedent matter of what the ground rules of the game
should be. The bulk of the discussion is devoted toward an argument about
the nature of the methodological approach we ought to take and the devel-
opment of desiderata that ought to constrain any ontology of the screen-
play we do develop.
My central argument is that any ontology of the screenplay must be con-
strained by the conceptions that are implicit in our creative and apprecia-
tive practices—in fact, that those practices determine the ontological status
of the screenplay. The consequence for the issues I raised in the preceding
paragraphs is this: Questions about the identity and existence conditions of
the screenplay are only answerable to the extent that answers are implicit in
our creative and appreciative practices. On one hand, this conclusion may
not seem particularly helpful. It will not, for example, provide us with a
clear-cut answer about how greatly two drafts of a screenplay must diverge
to count as two different screenplays.
On the other hand, my conclusion can offer substantive insights once we
begin unpacking the ontological assumptions implicit in our creative and ap-
preciative practices. One such assumption, which is of particular importance
to the present discussion, is that a distinction exists between work and text.
As I indicated in Chapters 1 and 2, a work such as a screenplay is a cultural
entity—a socio-historically situated artifact—that cannot be identified solely
with a text. If screenplays are not individuated merely by texts, then neither
is it the case that two instances of the same text are necessarily instances
of the same screenplay, nor is it the case that two slightly different texts
necessarily constitute different screenplays. My conclusion about the case
of State and Main is, | think, consistent with our creative and appreciative
practices: The Methuen edition constitutes an instance of Mamet’s text that

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 165
is moderately incorrect but still sufficiently accurate to furnish us with an
instance of the same work instanced by the text of the Newmarket edition.
PRELIMINARIES: REALISM AND MULTIPLE INSTANTIABILITY
At least three things seem immediately clear about the nature of Mamet’s
State and Main screenplay. First, the screenplay cannot be identified with
any particular copy of it. My Newmarket facsimile of the screenplay is not
a forgery of the screenplay, but a genuine instance of it. The second, related
point is that the screenplay cannot be identified as the original manuscript
because if that manuscript happened to be destroyed, the screenplay would
not cease to exist. On the contrary, I and anyone else who owns a Newmar-
ket edition would continue to have access to the screenplay, indicating that
the work survives the destruction of any particular copy of it—including the
original manuscript. Or, to put it another way, it seems obvious that the cast
and crew had access to the screenplay, but not by taking turns looking at the
original manuscript. Third, these points entail that it is not possible for the
screenplay to be identified with the total set of its copies, since the destruc-
tion or addition of a copy has no effect on the screenplay itself.
On the grounds of these three observations, it seems safe to say that a
screenplay is the kind of thing that is repeatable or capable of having mul-
tiple instances, but is not a physical object. Now, I imagine that this conclu-
sion will not strike philosophers as particularly noteworthy, but it may rub
against the intuitions of nonphilosophers—specifically, scholars of literature
and, in particular, screenwriting. Isn’t the materiality of the screenplay—its
particular font, margins, and spacing—an essential part of what it is? And
after all, is this not precisely why we have cause to wonder if the Methuen
edition is actually an instance of Mamet’s screenplay?
In fact, however, there is no tension between my suggestion that the
screenplay is not a physical object and the plausible claim that its peculiar
format is an essential part of its identity. Think, for example, of shaped or
concrete poems, which cannot be physical objects for the same reasons I have
suggested screenplays cannot be. Consider George Herbert’s “Easter Wings”:
Lord, Who createdst man in wealth and store,
Though foolishly he lost the same,
Decaying more and more,
Till he became
Most poore:
With Thee
O let me rise
As larks, harmoniously,
And sing this day Thy victories:
Then shall the fall further the flight in me.

166 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
My tender age in sorrow did beginne:
And still with sickness and shame
Thou didst so punish sinne,
That I became
Most thinne.
With Thee
Let me combine,
And feel this day Thy victorie,
For, if |i mp my wing on Thine,
Affliction shall advance the flight in me.®
Now, although what we might call the formatting of “Easter Wings” is
clearly an essential part of the poem’s identity, it does not follow that the
poem is actually a physical object. Suppose I memorize the entire poem
(both its words and layout) sometime before every copy of the poem is de-
stroyed. As long as I remember the poem well enough to produce a correct
copy of it, the poem exists despite the fact that no copies are extant. For
surely we do not want to say that the poem goes out of existence with the
destruction of all copies, yet I bring it back into existence when I create a
new copy from memory. On the contrary, the poem may continue to exist
even when it is not concretely instanced. Similarly, the screenplay, despite its
apparent “materiality,” may continue to exist (for example, in a PDF file)
even in the absence of any instances of it.
Again, this is not to deny that the screenplay’s formatting may be an
essential part of its identity. Although I want to refrain from drawing any
general conclusions about when and how elements of formatting constitute
essential properties of a screenplay, surely there are cases, such as that of
“Easter Wings,” in which one would be hard pressed to deny that some such
elements—say the margins—are indeed essential. Consider, for example,
this passage from Carl Mayer’s “Sunrise: A Song of Two Humans”:
Cows.
Returning to their stables.
Evening sun.
A couple from the city walking
along (with back toward camera.)’
In this case, the margins and line breaks are crucial to the creation of the
screenplay’s unique pacing. If these same four lines were reprinted within
standard screenplay margins, as a single block of prose, their rhythmic ef-
fect would be significantly diminished and the screenplay would be a lesser
work for it. So it is plausible that elements of formatting can constitute es-
sential properties of screenplays, but this does not require us to hold that
screenplays are physical objects. On the contrary, we have reasons to deny
that they are.

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 167
As the example of visual poetry indicates, the fact that screenplays are
not physical objects does not make them ontologically peculiar. They do
appear to differ ontologically from artworks like paintings and sculptures—
works that at least seem to be physical objects or concrete particulars. But
they seem ontologically similar to other sorts of artworks, such as novels
and poems as well as musical works and theatrical works. For like these
artworks, screenplays cannot be identified with any physical object (or set
thereof), but rather admit of multiple instances. With some notable excep-
tions, which I discuss presently, most contemporary philosophers of art ac-
cept this general distinction between artworks that are of the single-instance
and multiple-instance variety. The challenge of developing an ontology of
the screenplay is then, in large part, the broader one regarding the ontology
of multiple instance artworks: If a work cannot be identified as a concrete,
particular object, then as what is it to be identified?
If we reject what Richard Wollheim calls the “physical object hypoth-
esis”’—the view that all works of art are to be identified with concrete
particulars—and nominalism—the view that artworks are sets of concrete
particulars—it appears that there are essentially three ways we might think
about multiple-instance works such as screenplays: Multiple-instance works
(1) may exist in only in our minds, (2) may exist as independent abstract en-
tities, or (3) may not exist at all. The first option, idealism, is most famously
endorsed by Benedetto Croce and R. G. Collingwood.'° Collingwood, for
example, writes of music: “The tune is already complete and perfect when
it exists merely as a tune in [the composer’s] head . . . The music, the work
of art, is not the collection of noises, it is the tune in the composer’s head.”"!
Furthermore, he claims, “The work of art proper is something not seen or
heard, but something imagined.”'? More specifically, it is “an imagined
experience of total activity” that the artist has and that qualified viewers
reconstruct.!°
This sort of idealist conception of the work of art has been subjected to
serious criticism elsewhere and I will not review the arguments at length
here.'* The one objection I would emphasize is that if the artwork is “an
imagined experience,” then it becomes unclear how different people are
able to experience and refer to the exact same artwork. The “imagined ex-
perience” I reconstruct when listening to Arnold Schoenberg’s A Survivor
from Warsaw would presumably be quite different from that of a Holocaust
survivor from Warsaw, and it is not clear how either of these “imagined
experiences” could be the same as Schoenberg’s.’* This and other serious
objections to the idealist account make it a nonstarter at this stage in the
debate.
The third option, which comprises the view that multiple-instance works
do not exist, can take one of a couple distinct forms, including fictionalism
and eliminativism. Responding to this sort of ontological proposal would
require a separate essay, not to mention some heavy-duty lifting in meta-
physics. I have, in addition to these, other reasons to avoid dealing with this
kind of view here. One is that it is not a widely held view in the philosophy of

168 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
art, for good reason, I think.'® The particulars of various accounts aside, the
general idea that artworks do not exist runs so counter to our everyday cre-
ative and appreciative practices that it seems too implausible to even get off
the ground. This does not necessarily mean that the view is wrong, although
in what follows I make an argument for rejecting proposals that would
overturn the tacit ontological assumptions embedded in those practices.'”
The second option, which comprises a variety of realist proposals ac-
cording to which multiple instance artworks are some sort of independently
existing abstracta, is where the bulk of recent work on the ontology of art
has been done and is where I want to focus now. Of these various accounts,
the best-known subset might loosely be called type-token theories. Follow-
ing a distinction first made in philosophical aesthetics by Richard Rudner
and later propagated by Wollheim, these theories argue that repeatable
works of art should be thought of as types, which are instantiated through
their (multiple) tokens.!* What is usually debated is the nature of the type.
To take a more famous example from the literature, Peter Kivy holds that
composers discover rather than create their musical works, which are pure
sound structures of the eternal, platonic variety.'? On the other hand, Jer-
rold Levinson, while agreeing that musical works are types, insists that
they must be initiated types—that is, types that do not exist eternally, but
rather must be initiated by a particular person(s) at a particular time.?°
Other philosophers, however, have taken up realist positions that challenge
both type-token theories and the single-/multiple-instance distinction on
which they depend. Most notably, Gregory Currie has argued for a variety
of ontological monism, according to which works are “action types,” and
David Davies has argued that artworks are kinds of performances, or “ac-
tion tokens.”?!
Clearly, some of these theories are much more intuitively plausible than
are others. An initial question in an investigation of the ontology of the
screenplay, then, is this: What bearing should intuitive plausibility have on
our ontological theorizing? This question is manifested in specific terms
when we wonder, as I think we must, whether it is a problem for Barthe-
sian theories of the screenplay that they entail massive collective error
about the nature of a variety of literary works, including screenplays. Can
Barthesian critics be right about “texts” being endlessly rewritten if no-
body besides Barthesian critics treats them this way? Put in more general
terms, we need to ask a methodological question similar to the one we
asked with regard to defining the screenplay: To what extent is our ontol-
ogy of the screenplay to be descriptivist? To what extent is it beholden to
our actual practices and intuitions?
As in the context of defining the screenplay, I take it that we want to opt
for a strong version of descriptivism. Note here that while descriptivism
narrows the field of potential theories of the screenplay’s ontology, it still al-
lows for many different approaches. Nevertheless, this is a more contentious
position with regard to ontology than it is with regard to definition, and it

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 169
requires me to mount an argument for what I follow David Davies in calling
“a pragmatic constraint”—although I have something stronger in mind that
what Davies proposes.”
METHODOLOGY AND A CONSTRAINT
I want to argue that screenplays, like other works of art, must be the kinds
of things that we implicitly conceive them to be in our creative and appre-
ciative practices. Ultimately, the argument is motivated by the idea that, as
Jerrold Levinson puts it (writing about the ontology of music), works must
be individuated such that they can “bear the aesthetic and artistic attributes
we importantly ascribe to them. We have to conceive of them so that they
are what such attributions are of.” As Davies points out, there is also a
case to be made that this is how we should conceive of the ontology of art
in general. According to Davies, “the work of art, as a subject of inquiry in
the philosophy of art, is the unit of criticism and appreciation—the entity of
which we predicate the properties we ascribe in criticism and appreciation—
and the individuation of works must reflect this fact.”** In other words,
there is an argument to be made that if we think our practices are coherent,
then artworks, including screenplays, must be the sorts of things implied by
those practices.
There are, in addition, other good prima facie reasons for at least taking
our practices and intuitions under consideration in our attempts to develop
a theory of the screenplay’s ontology. As we saw in Chapter 1, in the context
of defining art, most philosophers of art agree that, rather than attempting
to delimit the boundaries of the concept, our theories must account for our
creative and appreciative practices. Furthermore, to the extent that our in-
tuitions about what counts as art are rooted in those practices, our theories
must also take into consideration our intuitions. It is not clear why our
theorizing about the sort of things artworks and, specifically, screenplays
are should be any different.
Of course, our creative and appreciative practices do not amount to a
robust definition or ontological theory and our pretheoretical intuitions
may require refinement, but if we accept that our practices and intuitions
constrain our definitions of art and art kinds, then we ought to accept that
those practices and intuitions also constrain our theories of the ontology of
art and art kinds. Andrew Kania has made this analogy:
[I]n working on a definition of art, a theorist may have some recourse
to claims about the value of art, but it would be peculiar if she set aside
our intuitions about the extension of the term “art”. She might suggest
revising our pre-theoretical intuitions about that extension, in the pro-
cess of working out her definition, but to exclude such intuitions from
the start would be to ignore the central data explained.”

170 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
A preliminary point, then, is that our philosophical work in the context of
defining art suggests that, prima facie, our philosophical work on the ontol-
ogy of art must take into account our practices and intuitions.
However, there is still a stronger argument to be made. For in the context
of the definitional project, it is not merely that our theories of art must take
into account our intuitions and practices. More important is the reason for
this: our creative and appreciative practices actually determine the exten-
sion of the concept “art.” Thus, inasmuch as (descriptive) definitions of art
attempt to correctly identify the concept’s extension, they fail if they cannot
account for all of our actual art practices. In this sense, practice is necessar-
ily primary, and the definitional project is parasitic upon it. We accept that it
is up to philosophers to work out definitions that line up with our practices
because those practices determine the extension of the concept “art” that
philosophers are trying to define.
The discussion in Chapter 2 suggested that this situation is not merely
matter of stipulation, but rather owes to the nature of artifact kinds.*° The
conclusion was that artisans determine the boundaries of artifact kinds
through their practices. For where someone has a substantive and substan-
tively correct conception of an artifact kind and successfully realizes her
attempts to make “one of those things,” her creation is one of those things;
it falls under the given artifact concept and is part of the concept’s exten-
sion.” If we accept that art is an artifact kind, then practitioners necessarily
determine its boundaries. Less contentiously and more to the point, inas-
much as the screenplay an artifact kind, practitioners necessarily determine
its boundaries.**
Underlying this view of artifact concepts sketched in Chapter 2 was Amie
L. Thomasson’s argument regarding the “qua problem” that plagues direct
theories of reference—at least as applied to artifact kinds.”? Direct refer-
ence theories generally hold that natural-kind terms acquire reference based
on a causal relationship to a set of objects that are individuated by their
underlying essence. In this type of theory of reference, a natural-kind term
such as “water” refers to all and only those things that have the underlying
structure H,O. This means that the relevant features required to belong to
the kind and the nature of the kind are empirically discoverable, indepen-
dent of our intuitions or beliefs. As philosophers Devitt and Sterelny put it,
“Users of a natural kind term need not have true beliefs about the underly-
ing nature of the relevant natural kind nor even have any beliefs sufficient
to identify its members.”*° For example, we may successfully refer to bats
even if we mistakenly believe that bats are not mammals. But a collective in-
tuition that bats are not mammals does not change the fact that they are; on
the contrary, empirical investigation can show that our intuition is wrong.
However plausible one takes direct theories of reference to be in the
context of natural kinds, artifact kinds raise the qua problem—namely,
that in attempting to give a term to an artifact kind, “the reference of the
speaker’s term will be radically indeterminate unless she disambiguates the

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 171
sort or category of kind she means to refer to.”*! That is, for example, if
I point to a stack of 90 pieces of paper and attempt to dub it “screenplay,”
I need to somehow pick out the “verbal-object-that-typically-suggests-
constitutive-elements-of-a-film” from the other sorts of things to which
I could be referring (paper stacked to a certain height; the biological kind
of the tree from which the paper was made; the paper’s molecules in their
present formation; an object of a weight appropriate for a doorstop). In this
case, reference cannot be established in virtue of a causal connection to a
mind-independent essence such as a chemical structure. On the contrary,
what does the necessary disambiguating work, according to Thomasson,
is the background conception of the features relevant to membership in a
kind-category and the nature of the kind held by those who ground and
reground the term.» As Devitt and Sterelny put it, “It seems that some-
thing about the mental state of the grounder must determine which putative
nature of the sample is the one relevant to the grounding.”* *
This does not suggest that grounders of the term “screenplay” have any-
thing like a sophisticated definition of the screenplay worked out, but it does
entail they are not susceptible to massive collective error about what counts
as a screenplay. For, in fact, it is their disambiguation in the grounding and
regrounding of the term that establishes the boundaries of the category. In
Thomasson’s words, this disambiguation “establishes the category of the
kind to be referred to (if the term refers at all), by establishing what sorts
of features are relevant to unifying the kind.”** Moreover, insofar as the
disambiguation of artifact-kind terms is achieved through the intentions of
artisans to create something of “that kind,” it is, more specifically, the inten-
tions of practitioners that establish the boundaries of the concept “screen-
play.” As Thomasson concludes, “‘makers’ concepts of what features are
relevant to being a member of the kind (whether function, shape, etc.) de-
termine what specific features (shape, function, etc.) are relevant to being
a member of a particular artifactual kind, and thus collectively determine
the boundaries of the kind and the kind’s specific ‘nature.’”** This much, I
hope, was established in Chapter 2, but what is important here is that the
argument has implications not only for defining the screenplay, but also for
theorizing its ontology.
The second part of Thomasson’s conclusion entails that practitioners not
only determine the boundaries of the kind “screenplay,” but also that they
and other grounders determine the facts about what kinds of things screen-
plays are. For the qua problem not only means that a grounder must have
a tacit understanding of what features are relevant to kind to which she
intends to refer, but must also, as Thomasson puts it, “have a background
conception of what ontological sort of art-kind he or she means the term
to refer to.”°° For example, to ground and reground the reference of the
term “screenplay,” one must have a tacit conception of the kind of thing a
screenplay is. To return to our earlier example, when I point my finger at
a 90-page stack of paper and call it “screenplay” and then point my finger

172 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
at a PDF file on my desktop and call it “screenplay,” I must already have a
background conception of the screenplay’s ontology—for example, I hold
the assumption that the screenplay is not a concrete particular, but rather
admits of multiple instances.
Furthermore, according to Thomasson, this kind of background onto-
logical conception “then disambiguates potential reference by determining
the ontological kind referred to by the art-kind term (if it succeeds in refer-
ring at all),” thus establishing rough persistence and identity conditions for
the kind.’” In other words, just as grounders’ collective conception of what
features are relevant to unifying a kind actually determine those features,
so, too, do grounders’ collective conception of a kind’s nature determine
its ontological status. Moreover, because the grounding and regrounding of
such terms is established, collectively, through “the practices of those who
use the terms and deal with the objects,” it is practitioners and others who
competently use and speak about a given kind that determine its ontologi-
cal nature.** In the case of the screenplay, it is people such as screenwriters,
studio executives, actors, crew members, screenwriting festival juries, and
casual readers of screenplays who ground and reground the reference of
the term “screenplay.” Thus, it is their collective background ontological
conception of the screenplay that determines what it is those things called
“screenplay” are like.
This does not mean that those responsible for grounding the reference
of artifact- or art-kind terms such as “screenplay” have fully developed
theories about the ontology of those kinds. Rather, background ontological
conceptions are implicit in their ordinary creative and appreciate practices
as well as in intuitions rooted in those practices. So, for example, my as-
surance that I am able to access Mamet’s State and Main screenplay via my
Newmarket copy of it rather than having to knock on his door and ask him
for it indicates that I have a tacit conception of the screenplay as a multiple-
instance work. So, too, does my belief that burning my copy will not destroy
the screenplay. Suppose that before burning my book, I scanned the screen-
play and saved it as a PDF file, and then all of the copies of the screenplay
did happen to be destroyed. My understanding that the screenplay has sur-
vived in a kind of code speaks to a tacit recognition that, although we access
screenplays through concrete instances of them, the screenplay’s existence
does not necessarily depend on the existence of one or more instances of it.
Furthermore, knowing that opening the PDF file to produce an instance of
the screenplay does not constitute “creating” it points to yet another com-
ponent of an implicit conception of the ontology of the screenplay.
Together, these sorts of beliefs and intuitions underlying our creative and
appreciate practices amount to a general picture of what kind of thing the
screenplay is ontologically—if not a fully developed theory. As Thomasson
summarizes, “such background practices thus embody a tacit ontological
conception of what sorts of things works of that kind are, which (under-
stood by grounders) disambiguates the reference of the relevant kind term,

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 173
and determines ontological features of the members of the kind (if any)
picked out by the term.”*? Therefore, although it would not make much
sense to ask Jerry Bruckheimer what he understands as the identity and ex-
istence conditions of the screenplay, the ordinary practices of Bruckheimer
and his ilk collectively reveal certain facts about the nature of the screenplay
that our theories must acknowledge. Indeed, if screenplays were actually
endlessly rewritten as the Barthesian critics would have it, one suspects that
high-powered producers such as Bruckheimer would not be paying script
doctors millions of dollars for a final “polish”!
Thus, when it comes to theorizing the ontology of the screenplay, as well
as defining of the screenplay, practitioners and other grounders have a kind
of epistemic privilege. Accounts of the ontology of art kinds that suggest
our collective intuitions could be radically mistaken suppose that there are
mind-independent facts about the nature of those art kinds. In what Thom-
asson calls “the discovery view of knowledge,” which is supported by direct
references theories, for any given question we might have about the nature
of the world, empirical investigation allows us to determine whether p or
not-p is the case, because there is a complete range of mind-independent
facts that are open to discovery.*° In the same way we can establish empiri-
cally that someone who thinks a bat is not a mammal is wrong, it may seem
possible that someone who denies that a screenplay is perpetually rewrit-
ten could also be wrong, despite the fact that such a theory is extremely
counterintuitive. Or, to put it less forcefully, we should not be surprised
or worried if according to this or that theory, some of our common-sense
beliefs about the nature of screenplays or other art kinds turn out to be in-
correct. But the preceding discussion suggests that pace the discovery view,
important facts about the nature of art and art kinds, which are not natural
kinds but cultural entities, are mind-dependent. More specifically, they are
determined by the ordinary creative and appreciative practices of people
who deal with and speak about screenplays. And insofar as our collective
intuitions about the ontology of the screenplay are based on the ordinary
creative and appreciative practices of people who deal with and speak about
screenplays, those intuitions are not subject to massive collective error.*!
It also follows that the ontological status of the screenplay and other art
kinds is not something that can be determined by “top down” general theo-
ries or by radically revisionist theories that do not line up with our practices
and intuitions. So, inasmuch as Barthesian poststructuralist theory does not
line up with our background practices and intuitions about the kinds of
things literary works and texts are, it does not offer an acceptable account
of their ontology. It simply cannot turn out to be the case that, despite the
fact that we do not treat them as such, screenplays or other texts are essen-
tially endlessly rewritten. At best, the proponent of such a revisionist theory
can claim that we should treat screenplays or other texts as if they are end-
lessly rewritten. But then the burden is on him to offer compelling reasons
to accept such a proposal.

174. A Philosophy of the Screenplay
In sum, the general methodological implication is this: We must abandon
implausible theories of the ontology of the screenplay or other art kinds
that do not line up with our ordinary creative and appreciative practices as
well as our collective intuitions based upon those practices. For, as Thomas-
son puts it, “the only appropriate method for determining [artworks’] on-
tological status is to attempt to unearth and make explicit the assumptions
about their ontological status built into the relevant practices and beliefs of
those dealing with works of art.”*? For us, what is needed to theorize the
ontology of the screenplay is to analyze the conceptions of the screenplay’s
nature that are implicit in the practices and intuitions of screenwriters and
other people who deal with and speak about screenplays. This is the nature
pragmatic constraint I propose.
DESIDERATA
Now, there is another benefit that I hope rehearsing Thomasson’s argument
has yielded: Given this broad methodological constraint, there are a number
of more specific desiderata that should require less argumentation than they
otherwise might. The reason for this is that the conditions I suggest are all
based on strongly held collective intuitions, which are, in turn, rooted in our
creative and appreciative practices. In addition, it is worth noting, they have
all been proposed and argued for elsewhere. In particular, I draw upon the
desiderata for an ontology of music proposed by Jerrold Levinson and the
desiderata for an ontology of art proposed by Peter Lamarque.**
Following upon the discussion that introduced this chapter, I propose
that any ontology of the screenplay must allow that screenplays are multiple
instantiability and real entities. | take it that I have already sufficiently set
out the reasons why we should accept the former claim. It should be recalled
that if screenplays were not multiple-instance works, then, for example, it
would not be possible for the cast and crew of a given production to have
access to the same screenplay unless they took turns looking at a single
manuscript. Clearly, however, photocopies of a screenplay suffice during
film production since they afford access to the screenplay in the same way
copies of novels, plays, or poems afford access to those works.
Moreover, it is evident that intersubjective access to the same screenplay
is possible; a screenplay does not merely reside in people’s minds, but exists
in the objective world. Thus, we should reject idealism about the screenplay.
While I have not offered an argument for rejecting fictionalism or elimina-
tivism, I think the descriptivism for which I have argued precludes us from
embracing either of those positions and, rather, commits us to realism.** So,
the first two desiderata are these: (1) Screenplays must be the kinds of things
that are multiply instantiable; (2) Screenplays must be real.
The third desideratum is creatability. By this, I mean that screenplays
must be the kinds of things that are brought into existence by their authors.

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 175
The belief that artists create their artworks is so intuitive that someone un-
familiar with recent debates in the ontology of art will probably wonder
how things could be otherwise. However, there is a variety of argument
that denies that multiple-instance artworks are created, and this must be
addressed because the first desideratum states that screenplays are multiply
instantiable. Very roughly, the argument runs as follows: Multiple instance
works cannot be identified with any physical object(s). In order to accom-
modate this fact, a prominent account of multiple-instance art, mentioned
earlier, holds that multiple-instance works are abstract types, which are in-
stantiated by tokens.** The problem is that, according to some such type-
token theories, because types are abstracta, which do not exist in space or
time, they are not the kinds of things that can be created.** Thus, in this
view, we are forced to conclude that writers and composers do not create
their works, but “discover” them.*”
Inasmuch as this account of the ontology of multiple-instance artworks
posits that artworks are not created, it is deeply counterintuitive, and I
think we have a number of good reasons to reject it. In the first place, it
ought to be clear that our broad methodological constraint proscribes any
such extremely counterintuitive theory. Beyond the arguments given earlier,
another way to motivate the constraint and insist upon the creatability of
screenplays and other artworks is to acknowledge Peter Lamarque’s point:
artworks are “cultural objects, dependent for their inception and survival
on cultural conditions; more specifically, they are entities essentially tied to
human acts and attitudes.”*® Thus, Lamarque concludes, as we also should,
works are created.
In addition, Jerrold Levinson offers a good argument for accepting a cre-
atability desideratum, which we can make use of here.” As he points out,
creatability is not any old intuition; it is perhaps the most inveterate and
fundamental view most people hold about art. Now, one response to this
claim is that it confuses creatability with creativity. According to this objec-
tion, this most foundational belief is actually just that artists are creative
people, but not that they necessarily create things. And, the objection goes,
this stands to reason because their works are more properly characterized as
“discoveries.” For example, Peter Kivy writes:
What I am suggesting is that it may be more plausible to think of [Mo-
zart’s| works as Platonic objects of some sort and, therefore, things that
could not have been brought into being . . . It takes nothing away from
Mozart’s creativity, in the important sense (or senses) of the word to
think of Mozart as a discoverer rather than as an inventor or creator.”
However, as Levinson argues, it is precisely the fact that we think of Don
Giovanni as Mozart’s creation that gives the opera and Mozart part of the
“status, significance, and value” they have. In his words, “We marvel at a
great piece of music in part because we marvel that, had its composer not

176 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
engaged in a certain activity, the piece would (almost surely) not exist; but
it does exist, and we are grateful to the composer for precisely that.”*' So,
too, I submit it is with literary works including poems, theatrical scripts,
and screenplays. Part of what gives the screenplay for High Noon (1952)
its status, significance, and value is our belief that a person, Carl Foreman,
created it. Moreover, we take it that the screenplay would not have existed
as it does were it not for the specific events that occurred while Foreman was
writing it and influenced its creation—namely, his being called to testify by
the House Committee on Un-American Activities.°*
Another problem for the argument against creatability is that it depends
on the questionable supposition that abstracta necessarily do not exist
in space or time. Recall that the general argument runs basically as fol-
lows: (1) Abstracta do not exist in space or time; (2) therefore, abstracta
are not created; (3) multiple-instance artworks are abstracta; (4) therefore,
multiple-instance artworks are not created. The first premise and the con-
clusions are commonly held views, but several philosophers have challenged
them by arguing that some abstracta necessarily exist temporally and, thus,
can be created.*? Without getting into the details of these technical meta-
physical arguments, we may mention that photographs appear to pose se-
rious problems for the notion that abstracta do not exist temporally. For
a photograph, if it exists, must be an abstract entity, since it cannot be
identified with any concrete particular such as a negative or a print. But if
photographs are abstract entities, they must be such that they exist in time,
because photographs are causally dependent on reality. For example, it ap-
pears implausible that a photograph of me, say, just after my birth, actually
existed before I was born. On the contrary, such a photograph appears to
be counterfactually dependent upon my existence; we want to say that the
photograph could not have existed until I was born—not that it already
existed and was merely waiting to be discovered. Another way of putting it
is this: it seems implausible that photographs are not created, because some
of the photograph’s artistic and aesthetic properties depend upon its specific
historical context; that is, it has properties that it could not have if it were
not created in that specific historical context.
For a similar reason, it is also implausible that other multiple-instance
works are not created. Again, if literary works and musical works exist, they
must be abstract entities. Yet such abstract entities must exist temporally
since, as several philosophers have argued, at least some of the works’ aes-
thetic properties depend upon their socio-historical context. Jerrold Levin-
son, for example, makes this point with regard to musical works. As he puts
it, “the aesthetic and artistic attributes of a piece of music are partly a func-
tion of, and must be gauged with reference to, the total musico-historical
context in which the composer is situated while composing his piece.”5 4O ne
example he offers is this: Although Brahms’s Piano Sonata op.2 (1852) is
clearly “Liszt-influenced,” if Beethoven had composed a work with exactly
same sound structure, it could obviously not have had the property of being

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 177
“Liszt-influenced” but would have had a “visionary quality” that Brahms’s
work does not.» Again, we see that the aesthetic properties of the work
depend upon a particular socio-historical context, which makes it deeply
implausible that the work is not created, but discovered. For if a work exists
eternally and is merely discovered, it is not at all clear how it possesses those
specific the socio-historically contingent aesthetic properties.
Despite Levinson’s insistence upon this principle, however, his account of
the ontology of musical works (and other multiple instance works) proposes
a principle of creatability that is not as strong as what we need here. It is
worth looking at his theory in some detail, both because it is one of the most
prominent alternatives to Platonist conceptions of multiple instance works,
and because it will be useful in specifying a stronger sense of creatability for
our desideratum. Levinson maintains that in addition to the pure structures
normally associated with types, there is another kind of abstract structure—
an indicated structure—that is a type of a different class. In Levinson’s view,
pure structures belong to a class of type he calls “implicit,” while indicated
structures belong to a class of type he calls “initiated.” According to Levin-
son, “Implicit types include all purely abstract structures that are not incon-
sistent—e.g., geometrical figures .. . The other class of types, initiated types,
are so called because they begin to exist only when they are initiated by an
intentional human act of some kind.”°** For Levinson, a musical work is an
initiated type—more specifically, a performed-sound structure-as-indicated-
by-X-at-t where X is the composer and tf is the time of composition.*” The
performed sound structure is a pure sound structure conjoined with a spe-
cific means of performance. In this view, however, the composer does not
bring into being, strictly speaking, the performed sound structure. Rather,
it is through the action of indicating the performed sound structure at a par-
ticular time that the composer brings into being the performed sound struc-
ture as indicated by her at a particular time—and this is a musical work.
The relevance for us is that Levinson thinks this account of the ontology
of musical works can be applied to literary works, as well. This is because he
views literary works as indicated word sequence structures that are initiated
by a particular author at a particular time. “Musical works,” he writes, “are
indicated structures . . . and thus types that do not already exist but must
instead be initiated. The same is true of poems, plays, and novels—each
of these is an entity more individual and temporally bound than the pure
verbal structure embodied in it.”** And, presumably, the same is also true
of screenplays. Levinson wants to deny that literary works are pure verbal
structures for the same reason he wants to deny that musical works are pure
sound structures: the artistic and aesthetic attributes of each are in part de-
pendent on the context of their creation. Hence, he claims:
[A] poem is certainly not just a given word sequence. A poem is the
product of a particular individual at a specific time and place, with a
reasonably definite meaning and aesthetic character that is in part a

178 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
function of that time and place. A word sequence per se, on the other
hand, existing as long as the relevant language has existed, cannot be
the creation of a given person and possesses neither the sort of meaning
nor the aesthetic character a poem bears in its context. By analogy with
my proposal for musical works, I would be inclined to regard a poem of
the standard sort as a word structure-as-indicated-by-X-at-t.””
Thus, in Levinson’s account of the ontology of literary works, a screenplay
would be, roughly, a word structure-as-indicated-by-a screenwriter X-at-a
time t. Despite the merits of this proposal, it suffers from two problems
relevant to our present discussion—one of which I will note here and one of
which I will detail shortly.
In terms of the creatability desideratum, the trouble with the work as
indicated structure/initiated type view is that initiating or indicating a work
is not the same as creating it in an ordinary sense. For if the writer is to
indicate the structure, then the pure structure, the word sequence, already
exists—as Levinson admits. That is, the writer does not create the word
sequence, but indicates it at a time t. One wonders, though, how much
difference there really is between X indicating a word sequence at t and X
discovering a word sequence at t. As I see it, Levinson’s stating of the theory
in this particular manner, alongside the claim that a pure word sequence
cannot be the creation of a person, is part of what Andrew Kania identifies
as the theory’s appeal to metaphysical respectability.®
The problem is that, parsed this way, the theory violates our intuitions
nearly as much as the platonic theories Levinson seeks to avoid in the first
place. For it does not seem plausible that the word sequence of a work
such as James Joyce’s Ulysses or Foreman’s screenplay for High Noon ex-
isted as soon as the English language existed, and that Joyce and Foreman
have indicated these structures at a particular time rather than created them.
How would this account handle a case such as Finnegan’s Wake, in which
language seems to be invented in the work? The intuitions that our theories
must respect are not these writers have indicated the verbal structures of
their works, but actually created them in the ordinary sense of the term.
Indeed, this seems to be part of the reason a work such as Finnegan’s Wake
has the status it does. In short, Levinson’s argument may be a victory for the
creationist, but it comes at the cost of having to, nevertheless, accept a ver-
sion of the fundamentally counterintuitive view we wanted to reject in the
first place—namely, that there is still a sense in which the artist picks out an
already existing pure structure. This, I submit, is not as strong a notion of
creatability as is required by our broad methodological constraint.
In sum, although it seems plausible that multiple-instance works, includ-
ing screenplays, are abstract, it does not appear to be the case that they
do not exist temporally and, therefore, are not created. The examples of-
fered above indicate that multiple-instance works are necessarily situated
in specific socio-historical contexts and, therefore, cannot be the kinds of

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 179
things that exist eternally. Moreover, denying that things such as photo-
graphs, symphonies, and screenplays are created comes at enormous cost
to the ways in which we normally engage with, think about, and speak
about them. Finally, if we respect our methodological constraint, we need
an ontology of the screenplay that recognizes that screenplays are created in
the ordinary sense of “created.” Therefore, the second desideratum is this:
Screenplays must be the kinds of things that are creatable and, furthermore,
do not exist until they are created (in the ordinary sense of the word) by
their author(s).
These reflections on the historical contingency of multiple-instance art-
works that have come out of a defense of the creatability requirement moti-
vate a fourth desideratum for any ontology of the screenplay. This condition,
borrowed—as several of the preceding points are—from Jerrold Levinson,
is that of “fine individuation.”*! In the context of music, Levinson explains
the fine individuation constraint as follows: “Musical works must be such
that composers composing in different musico-historical contexts [including
a single composer on separate occasions] who determine identical sound
structures invariably compose distinct musical works.”® Amie Thomasson
makes a similar proposal with regard to literature: “a work of literature de-
pends rigidly on the acts of its particular author to exist, so that, even if two
authors coincidentally composed the same words in the same order, they
would not thereby have composed the same work of literature.”® | sug-
gest we bring a similar condition to bear upon the ontology of the screen-
play: Screenplays must be the kinds of things whose identities depend upon
the actions of one or more particular writers in a specific socio-historical
context such that two writers who compose the same text do not thereby
compose the same screenplay. More generally, what I am proposing here is
a work/text distinction, to which I alluded at the beginning of this chapter.
This desideratum seems especially important in the context of the screen-
play because, as I also mentioned previously, if one supposes that works are
just texts and one assumes a sufficiently narrow conception of textual iden-
tity, we will be faced with an extraordinary proliferation of screenplays. The
two editions of Mamet’s State and Main screenplay would, for example,
turn out to be two different screenplays if one decided they were not tokens
of the same text-type. Or one could argue that the standard practice of re-
writing a screenplay is always an exercise in the creation of many different
screenplays, never a process in the creation of just one. As a consequence,
speaking of “the” screenplay of a film would be always and necessarily
nonsensical. Such conclusions are too far at odds with our practices and
intuitions, I think, to be acceptable. To be sure, however, there are some phi-
losophers who have sophisticated accounts of work-identity according to
which a literary work is reducible to a text. Nelson Goodman, for example,
writes, “Merely by determining that the copy before us is spelled correctly
can we determine that it meets all requirements for the work in question.”
In other words, the thought is that the identity of a work is determined by

180 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
the text, so compliance with the text is enough to identify a genuine instance
of the work. Work and text are the same. Call this view “textualism.”*
An oft-cited example held to refute textualism is Jorge Luis Borges’s short
story “Pierre Menard, Author of the Quixote,” in which the narrator re-
views the life’s work of Pierre Menard, a fictional twentieth-century French
author, who “rewrote,” word-for-word, certain parts of Miguel Cervantes’s
Don Quixote. Despite the fact that parts of the two texts are identical, the
narrator attributes different aesthetic properties to each. “The contrast in
style,” claims the narrator, “is... vivid. The archaic style of Menard—quite
foreign, after all—suffers from a certain affectation. Not so that of his fore-
runner, who handles with ease the current Spanish of his time.”®* The fre-
quently drawn conclusion is that because we seem to have here two tokens
of a single-text type and two distinct works (differing in aesthetic proper-
ties), a literary work cannot be identified solely with a text (or text-type).°”
Recent work in aesthetics has arguably been more circumspect in ex-
tracting philosophical implications from this example.®* Of course, one may
accept that Menard and Cervantes have written the same text, but neverthe-
less deny they have written two different works. This was Goodman’s move
some years ago and there is, as Peter Lamarque notes, evidence within the
story to suggest that Borges intends us to be amused by the absurdity of his
narrator’s conclusion that Menard has created a distinct work.”
In addition, as Paisley Livingston points out, the story’s implications for a
text/work distinction—and, indeed, the plausibility of that distinction more
generally—depend on how one conceives of a text.” It is, for example, plau-
sible that on a speech-act theoretical account of textual identity, Menard has
produced a distinct text as well as a distinct work, in which case the story
fails to illustrate a work/text distinction.”! Thus, part of what is needed to
argue for the work/object distinction and against textualism is a plausible
account of textual identity.
A preliminary matter, however, is to deny that an extremely narrow view
of textual identity, on which the two editions of the Mamet screenplay
would be two different texts (and, thus, potentially two different works),
is tenable. Surely it is too much of a stretch to say that someone who has
read the Methuen edition of State and Main, which contains changes to the
font, type, and spacing of Mamet’s text, has not actually read Mamet’s text
at all. Methuen would have quickly had a lawsuit on its hands if it were
the case that what it printed and sold under the guise of Mamet’s text was
something else altogether. Rather, it seems that readers of the Methuen edi-
tion have encountered an incorrect or ill-formed instance of Mamet’s text.
Layout and formatting are surely important elements of the screenplay text,
but sameness of layout and formatting cannot be a necessary condition for
two text-tokens to be of the same text-type.
We need a less restrictive account of textual identity, but neither develop-
ing nor defending one is within my purview here. For the present purpose,
then, let us assume that something like the proposals offered by Paisley

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 181
Livingston and Peter Swirski are plausible. In Art and Intention, Livingston
develops what he calls the “locutionary” account of textual identity. In Liv-
ingston’s view, “Reference to [the author(s)| intentions is necessary... to the
identification of a collection of sentences constitutive of the primary token
constitutive of a locutionary text.”” In Literature, Analytically Speaking,
Swirski offers a similar proposal, according to which “the same text is one
that embodies its author’s executive intentions, allowing for textual variants
occasioned by the evolving design, modernization, correction of unintended
scriptural elements, and so forth.””?
Roughly speaking, the sort of account we ought to adopt regards au-
thorial intentions as determinative of the identity of a text-type. In such a
view, text-tokens that conform sufficiently to the same intended characters,
words, sentences, and, in some cases, fonts, line breaks, and so forth, will
count as instances of the text-type—regardless, incidentally, of their causal
provenance.” The qualifier “sufficiently” opens to door to hard and, per-
haps, indeterminable cases, but it also allows for some margin of error—
which we ordinarily accept—in a given text-token’s matching the primary
token. When a given text-token does not replicate all and only those in-
tended characters of the primary token, we do not necessarily conclude that
it is not a token of the same text-type as the primary token, but rather adju-
dicate the matter with an eye to whether it sufficiently matches the primary
token.
With at least a rough conception of textual identity in hand, what I
want to emphasize as a motivation for both the text/work distinction and
the fine individuation desideratum is the socio-historical embeddedness of
the act of artistic creation. Consider, then, the following examples, which
raise dilemmas for textualism: First, Preston Sturges wrote the screenplay
for The Power and the Glory (1933). The screenplay is regarded as highly
original for its use of flashbacks as a device to reconstruct a character’s
past.”° But the exact same screenplay written in 1942, after the production
of Citizen Kane (1941), would lack such inventiveness. Indeed, it could not
have influenced Mankiewicz and Welles’s screenplay in the way one might
reasonably suspect it did. Second, Charles Lederer’s screenplay for His Girl
Friday (1940) contains dialogue that was, for its time, naturalistic. In an
identical contemporary screenplay, however, the dialogue would be stilted
and contrived.
One might object these examples depend on historically evolving stan-
dards and conventions rather than on any properties that the screenplays
themselves have. That is, it might be claimed that attributes such as “origi-
nal” or “naturalistic” are relational properties not intrinsic to the screen-
plays themselves. I think this misses the point, but in any case, here are two
further examples to head off such concerns. Todd Haynes’s screenplay for
Far From Heaven (2002), written in 2002 but set in 1957, is a Douglas Sirk-
influenced ironic melodrama. The opening scene gives a good sense of the
screenplay’s tone:

182. A Philosophy of the Screenplay
HARTFORD, CONNECTICUT, 1957
EXT. NEW ENGLAND VILLAGE—DAY
The bells of a Gothic church are ringing down upon a comely square
near downtown Hartford. The crisp fall day is alive with color, as
cornflower skies burst through the autumn reds and golds, setting off
the clean brick buildings and freshly painted homes that so proudly
distinguish this New England township.”°
Now this screenplay would have been extremely difficult to shoot in
1950s Hollywood because of its candid portrayal of homosexuality. But
suppose the papers of a 1950s Hollywood screenwriter—say, Hank Todd—
are discovered tomorrow and it just so happens among them is a screenplay
written in 1957 that is identical to Todd Haynes’s screenplay. Suppose fur-
ther that, owing to some notes he has left behind, we know that Hank Todd
intended his screenplay to be an earnest melodrama in the vein of Stanley
Kramer’s social problem films. It seems clear, in this case, that despite the
fact that the texts of Todd Haynes’s screenplay and Hank Todd’s screenplay
are the same, Hank Todd’s work does not have the ironic tone—and cer-
tainly not the Sirk-influenced irony—that Haynes’s work has.
Finally, imagine that “Unforgiven (The William Munny Killings)”—the
screenplay eventually revised and used to shoot Unforgiven (1992)—was
written not by David Webb Peoples in the late 1970s, but by Frank Nugent
in the mid 1950s before he wrote the screenplay for The Searchers (1956).
Nugent’s “Unforgiven (The William Munny Killings)” would be nihilistic
on the heels of his screenplays for She Wore a Yellow Ribbon (1949) and
Wagonmaster (1950), yet before the exploration of darker material in The
Searchers screenplay. On the other hand, if we imagine that in the 1970s,
Peoples wrote what we know as the screenplay for The Searchers, that work
would be tame and quaint following shortly after Walton Green and Sam
Peckinpah’s screenplay for The Wild Bunch (1969).
These examples suggest that two instances of the exact same text are
not necessarily instances of the same screenplay, and screenplays cannot
be identified with pure word-sequence types or text-types.”” As Thomasson
puts it, “By virtue of originating in a different place in literary, social, and
political history, at the hands of a different author, or a different place in
an author’s oeuvre, one and the same sequence of words can provide the
basis for two very different works of literature with different aesthetic and
artistic properties.”’”* Therefore, we should accept both the work/text dis-
tinction and the “fine individuation” requirement, making the appropriate
modifications to the latter so that it applies to screenplays: Screenplays must
be such that screenwriters writing in different socio-historical contexts who
compose identical texts invariably compose distinct screenplays.
The fifth desideratum I propose is tied to the creatability restriction.
Screenplays must be things that are not only brought into existence by their

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 183
authors, but that can go out of existence. This restriction is worth making
explicit because anyone who denies that multiple instance works are created
on the grounds that they are abstracta, without spatial or temporal existence,
will also deny, on the same grounds, that they can be destroyed. However, our
ordinary practices and beliefs indicate that, on the contrary, multiple-instance
works, including screenplays, are the kinds of things that can be destroyed.
We do not need to repeat all of the objections to platonism that we made
in the context of creatability. Suffice it to say that, again, the methodological
constraint ought to ensure that screenplays are the kinds of things that are
destructible. However, Thomasson has noted one additional challenge to
the kind of destructibility proposed here.”? Often, when a literary work is no
longer accessible to us because no instances of it exist and no one can recall
a correct instance of it from memory, we say that the work is lost. Does this
not indicate that we merely no longer have access to the work—not that it
is actually destroyed? I doubt this is the case. On the contrary, the implica-
tion that underlies our use of the word “lost” instead of “destroyed” is that,
because the advent of the printing press and other technologies have made it
easy to produce many occurrences of multiple-instance artworks, it is hard
to be sure that all instances of a work have, in fact, been destroyed and that
the work is truly unrecoverable.
But when we say that a multiple-instance artwork is “lost,” this does not
mean we actually doubt that the work could be destroyed—that if, in fact,
the work is not recoverable, it continues to exist in some platonic ether,
waiting to be (re)discovered. Rather, the “lost” locution just owes to the
impossibility of definitively determining that no instances of a particular,
previously existing work still exist. As Thomasson argues, this idea “is rein-
forced by noting that, although we ordinarily speak of old or ancient works
as lost, in the case of a modern manuscript burned by its author, we are
more prone to count the work as ‘destroyed’ than merely lost.”*® Thus, I
submit, no great violence to intuition is done in saying that Joyce’s first play,
A Brilliant Career, was destroyed and ceases to exist, since we know that he
himself destroyed the only copy.*! We do not say that a work is “lost” rather
than “destroyed” to exclude this sort of possibility, but rather to indicate
our uncertainty about the existence of any extant instances of a work.
So, I claim that there is no surprising, platonic ontological commitment
behind our references to “lost” works. In some cases, we do believe that all
instances of the work have been lost, but hope or suspect that one may be
found. In other cases, though, we use the locution even though we know
that all instances have been destroyed and the work no longer exists, and in
these cases the locution is nothing but a colloquial way of saying just that. In
these sorts of cases in which an author destroys the manuscript or other sole
extant instance of a literary work and we say the work is “lost,” we use the
term figuratively—as when we say a doctor has “lost” a patient.*
Our practices, however, commit us to the notion that literary works—
including screenplays—can and do go out of existence. We hope, for

184 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
example, that the manuscript of Sylvia Plath’s Double Exposure is in fact
only lost precisely, because otherwise the work no longer exists. We main-
tain archives in part so that sole instances of such works are kept safe from
destruction and we mourn the loss of works that are destroyed. For as long
as one instance of Mamet’s State and Main screenplay survives, then the
work exists. However, once every instance of that screenplay is destroyed
and there is no possible way of generating a new instance of it (e.g., if it is
scanned and saved as a PDF file that could generate an instance), the work
ceases to exist. So, the fourth desideratum is this: Screenplays must be the
kinds of things that can go out of existence.
However, we see here that screenplays raise particular questions that it
may seem other literary works do not. Indeed, we have come full circle to
the question of identity with which the chapter began. It is fine to say that
Mamet’s State and Main screenplay survives as long as there is an instance
of it, but what counts as an instance? If a copy of the Methuen edition
were all_we had left, would we say that we had an instance of Mamet’s
screenplay or would we say that the work was lost? What about earlier
drafts of the screenplay—would they count as instances? How close would
an earlier draft have to be to the final shooting script to count as an in-
stance? I will come to these questions shortly.
But first, let us address the question of whether Mamet’s screenplay sur-
vives not in virtue of any copy of it surviving, but in virtue of a copy of the
film, State and Main surviving. Intuitively, one might want to say that the
screenplay survives as long as a copy of the film survives. For, it might be
thought, such a situation would be equivalent to a case in which film nega-
tive, while not an instance of a film, provides a means by which an instance
of the film could be generated. Is it not also the case that, although a film
is not an instance of a screenplay, it might provide a means by which an
instance could be produced?
No. A screenplay cannot survive solely in virtue of the survival of a
film made from it. For as we saw in Chapter 6, the screenplay has proper-
ties the film does not, and the film has properties the screenplay does not.
There is a rigid causal relationship between a film and its negative: the
negative is a template from which an instance of the film is generated.™
But no such relationship exists between a screenplay and a film. For ex-
ample, any negative (in acceptable condition) of High Noon will neces-
sarily generate a correct instance of the film. However, if Clint Eastwood
(or anyone else) shoots Carl Foreman’s screenplay, the result with not be
an instance of the 1952 film, High Noon, but a different film altogether.
High Noon survives through its various templates—its negatives, prints,
DVD copies, and so forth—but it will not survive if all of these things
are destroyed and only Carl Foreman’s screenplay survives because Fore-
man’s script cannot generate a correct instance of it. Shooting Foreman’s
screenplay may generate a work that resembles High Noon, but is a dif-
ferent work.

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 185
Working backwards, things are the same: Watching High Noon, some-
one might be able to reconstruct a screenplay that resembles Foreman’s,
but that screenplay will necessarily not be the same as Foreman’s. The film
High Noon cannot tell us exactly what settings, costumes, camera angles
and movements, edits, sound effects, dialogue, and so forth that Foreman’s
screenplay suggested. Or, more precisely, the film cannot tell us anything
about the language Foreman used to suggest the film’s constitutive elements.
And that language is an essential part of the identity of the screenplay. But
even if someone, somehow, watched High Noon and composed a text that
was identical to Carl Foreman’s screenplay, that person cannot have thereby
produced an instance of Foreman’s screenplay. For, as we have seen, a liter-
ary work such as a screenplay “depends rigidly on the acts of its particular
author to exist.”** Thus, survival of the film High Noon does not constitute
the survival of Carl Forman’s screenplay “High Noon.” Nor does the sur-
vival of any film entail the survival of a screenplay made from it.
CONCLUSION: BACK TO THE QUESTION
OF IDENTITY CONDITIONS
Finally, we return to the matter of the screenplay’s identity via the ques-
tion of what it takes to destroy a screenplay. This chapter started by asking
whether the Methuen edition is a genuine instance of Mamet’s screenplay,
and that question is brought into sharp relief by asking whether or not the
screenplay would survive if all we had was a copy of the Methuen edition.
The related questions are whether the screenplay could survive through a
different draft or version of it, and, if so, how different another draft or ver-
sion could be before we were unwilling to say that it constituted an instance
of the screenplay.
But although some of these questions may admit of answers, we can
now see that some are simply unanswerable. If the ontological status of
an art-kind like the screenplay is determined by our practices and the col-
lective intuitions rooted in those practices—not discoverable through any
investigation other than the analysis of them—we must conclude that such
questions are only answerable to the extent that our practices and collec-
tive intuitions provide answers. As Thomasson writes, “since facts about
the ontology of the work of art are determined by human conceptions, the
resulting facts are, as we might say, ontologically shallow—there is noth-
ing more to discover about them than what our practices themselves deter-
mine.”®> Thus, where our practices do not indicate sharp identity conditions
for screenplays, we ought to accept that there are no discoverable answers
to general questions about those conditions.
What does this mean for our practical problems with regard to State
and Main? The issue of whether the Methuen edition counts as an instance
of the work does appear to be decidable. In the preceding discussion, I

186 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
attempted to head off the worry that the Methuen edition is not an instance
of Mamet’s text; to deny that it is so assumes too strict a conception of
textual identity. But if the Methuen edition is an instance of the same text-
type as the Newmarket edition, there is no compelling reason, in this case,
to doubt that it is also an instance of the same work. More accurately, the
Methuen edition is an incorrectly formed instance of the text and the work,
which, I think, would still continue to exist if the Methuen edition was all
we had. This is not to suggest that we ought to conceive of screenplays as
“norm-kinds,”** but merely that in our standard practices we do acknowl-
edge improperly formed instances of literary works (and musical works) as
genuine work-instances.
At the same time, however, we also accept that some would-be work in-
stances diverge so greatly from the work that they do not count as instances.
If a given text is unrecognizable as an instance of a particular work, I take it
we do not regard it as an instance. Naturally, the difficult question is where
exactly that line is: At what point does a given text count as an instance of
the screenplay and at what point does it not? How ill-formed must an in-
stance of a screenplay be to fail to count as one?
Unfortunately, I do not see that our practices give us much of an an-
swer. However, there are at least two important points to take away here.
First, it is evident that we are able to and do adjudicate matters on a
case-by-case basis, even if the identity and existence conditions implicit
in Our practices are fuzzy. The fact that our practices admit no general
account of those conditions does not, though, lead us to conclude with
the poststructuralists that work identity (or textual identity) is completely
fluid. On the contrary, it is evident that we have intersubjective access to
the same works and that we are usually able to agree about when we have
read the same work. Our practices indicate, perhaps, that the facts about
the identity conditions of screenplays are, as Thomasson puts it, “onto-
logically shallow”*’—not, as the poststructuralists might have it, that they
are ontologically hollow.
The second point is that these sorts of philosophical quandaries regard-
ing textual identity and work identity are hardly specific to the screenplay.
As Peter Swirski puts it, discussing literature more broadly, “The mother of
all questions is how much difference makes a difference—in other words,
how much textual deviation flips over to another text? Notwithstanding
millennia of editorial praxis and an inundation of theory, the long and short
answer is that there is no answer.”** Because screenplays tend to be rewritten
more frequently, written more collaboratively, written more publicly, and,
perhaps, completed less often than are other literary works such as poems
and novels, it might seem at first glance that there are important ontological
difference between the forms. It might initially appear that the screenplay is
more fluid, less stable, incomplete, or ontologically suspect in some similar
way. But although contingent industrial facts about screenwriting may bring
philosophical questions about work identity, textual identity, and the text/

Toward an Ontology of the Screenplay 187
work relationship into sharp relief, it is implausible that they raise substan-
tive questions not already raised by the works of Beckett, Dickinson, Shake-
speare, Whitman, and many other literary artists whose work was written
(and rewritten) in a variety of complex art-industrial contexts.
Rather, after critical reflection, it is plausible that the screenplay is very
much like works such as poems, theatrical scripts, and novels—both in-
asmuch as it has a defensible claim to literary status and insofar as it is
ontologically similar to these works. This is not to suggest that there are no
substantive ontological differences between different sorts of literary works.
Robert Howell has raised forceful objections to this idea and argued for
embracing a kind of pluralism.*? In the present context, it is worth empha-
sizing that my arguments are compatible with his claim that there are many
literary works that do seem to have relatively fluid identities—at least com-
pared to works whose identities essentially involve a fixed text of some sort.
Traditional stories, fairy tales, myths, and oral epics, Howell points out,
“are not defined in terms of fixed textual sequences but rather in terms of
determinate (but often still somewhat fluid) plot sequences that are verbally
expressed .. . The nature of these works is falsified if you try to force them
into the fixed-text framework.””? We can, however, accept this sort of local
pluralism about literary ontology Howell proposes, while still maintaining
that there are many literary works—especially in popular, contemporary
forms—whose identities are at least partly determined by a fixed text and
that are ontologically alike.
My claim, then, is not that the screenplay is ontologically the same as
literary works in general, but rather that it is ontologically similar to liter-
ary works in the sorts of forms with which it is generally contemporaneous.
In particular, it is plausible that the screenplay is the same kind of thing
ontologically as are short stories, novels, theatrical scripts, and text-based
poems. What kind of thing is this, specifically? As we have seen, there is no
generally accepted answer to this question, and I am happy to let metaphysi-
cians hash out the details. Amie L. Thomasson’s proposal, which strikes me
as attractive and which seems to avoid the dilemma Howell raises, tries to
account for the ontological status of all literary works, and multiple-instance
works generally, by identifying them as “abstract artifacts.”’' Roughly, the
idea here is that literary works are one of several varieties of abstracta that
depend on the actions of individuals situated in a particular socio-historical
context. In her words, “although these entities are abstract in the sense of
lacking a spatiotemporal location, many of them depend upon contingent
entities . . . Moreover, abstract artifacts are not timeless, but, instead, are
created at a particular time in particular circumstances, can change, and can
once again cease to exist even after they have been created.””* This, I recog-
nize, is a controversial proposal, and a defense of it is beyond my purview.
Its appeal, however, is that it meets the conditions that I have argued must
apply to any ontology of the screenplay. Whatever they are, screenplays
must be the kinds of things that are:

188 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
1. multiply instantiable—screenplays must admit of multiple instances;
2. real—screenplays must be real, rather than ideal or fictional, entities;
3. creatable—screenplays must not exist until they are created by their
writer(s);
4. finely individuable—screenplays must be such that screenwriters
writing in different socio-historical contexts who compose the same
text compose distinct screenplays;
5. destructible—screenplays must no longer exist when neither any of
their instances nor the capacity for generating a new instance exists.
Beyond the methodological constraint and these desiderata, the nature of
the screenplay, it seems to me, is open for debate. But I leave to those whose
expertise lies in the metaphysics of art this topic and, more specifically, the
question of whether screenplays are best thought of as special kinds of cre-
atable and destructible types, as abstract artifacts, or as some other kind of
entities that meet the above desiderata.

Part IV
Appreciation

2 The Appreciation of the Screenplay
as Literature
If, as I have argued, at least some—and perhaps many—screenplays are lit-
erary works, one may justly wonder what bearing this has on how we read
them. What is it, one may ask, to read the screenplay as literature? Two ini-
tial suppositions might seem natural here. First, one might assume that there
is an essential divide between reading a screenplay qua literary work and
qua set of instructions for the production of a film. It seems unlikely that
various members of a production team read a screenplay in the same fash-
ion that students might read it in an English class. And perhaps this raises
the concern that reading the screenplay as a literary work is inappropriate
because it seems to extract the screenplay from its production context.! Sec-
ond, and relatedly, one might suppose we need no special account of our
appreciative practices with regard to screenplays because a general account
of those practices in the context of literature will suffice. In other words,
it may seem that once the screenplay is categorized as literature, questions
about how we read the screenplay dissolve into broader questions about
how we read literature.
However, neither of these assumptions is warranted. One mistake com-
mon to both is the presumption that our reading and appreciation of litera-
ture are monolithic, uniform practices. On the contrary, they are clusters of
practices that provide the means of making relevant distinctions between
different categories of literature on the basis of form, genre, intended func-
tion, and so forth. Very few scholars or philosophers of literature would, I
think, suggest that a Spenserian sonnet and a modernist novel afford just the
same sorts of appreciation. Plausibly, two such works of literature must be
approached differently because they sit in distinct subcategories, partly as
a result of the authors’ intentions about the kinds of works they were mak-
ing and the art-historical contexts in which they were working.” Similarly,
