a screenplay is embedded in a distinct literary practice, one that has its own
internal norms and conventions, its own purposes and goals, and its own
modes of appreciation, which have developed alongside the practice. So,
it is not the case that categorizing the screenplay (or some screenplays) as
literature obviates the need for a specific account of what it is to appreciate
the screenplay as literature. Questions about the appreciation of screenplays

192 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
cannot simply be reduced to questions about the appreciation of literature
more broadly.
It is also far from clear that reading the screenplay qua literature is an
essentially different enterprise from reading the screenplay qua production
plan. For one may admit that reading a screenplay in a production context
is not the same in every way as reading a screenplay outside of a production
context while maintaining that these kinds of readings are similar in all the
relevant ways. I do not read Mother Courage in the same way as does Fiona
Shaw when she prepares to play the title role, but this hardly shows that
we are not both reading the script qua literature. Of course, whether we
are depends on how one replies to the question, “What is it to appreciate
the screenplay as literature?” And this is further reason to try to develop a
response.
This chapter offers some initial considerations for further work on the
appreciation of screenplays as works of literature. Despite the hesitancy of
many scholars of screenwriting to explicitly write about the screenplay as
literature, some relevant work on this topic has already been done under
the guise of studying the screenplay as “text.”? My goal here is to make
a twofold contribution to this body of work. I want show what bearing
my account of the screenplay’s ontology has upon its appreciation, and I
want to examine one particular component of screenplay reading that is
common to the appreciation of other literary works, yet of particular im-
portance to the appreciation of the screenplay. The chapter proceeds in this
way: First, I draw upon Part III’s discussion of ontology for the purposes of
setting down some basic conditions for the proper appreciation of screen-
plays. Second, I argue that reading the screenplay qua literary work and
reading the screenplay qua production plan are mutually compatible.
Part of the proper appreciation of the screenplay, I suggest, is attending
to the categorical intentions of the writer(s), which requires the reader to
remain cognizant of production context. Third, I attempt to adumbrate a
characteristic—if not essential—way in which we engage with screenplays
(whether or not in production contexts) as literary works: typically, the con-
juring of mental imagery figures centrally in the reading of a screenplay.
ONTOLOGICAL MATTERS
With regard to the appreciation of screenplays, the central conclusion to be
drawn my discussion of the screenplay’s ontology is this: The screenplay is
not the kind of thing that can be properly appreciated by watching a film;
rather, at least one part of the proper appreciation of a screenplay involves
reading it. Here I assume, with David Davies, that a necessary condition
for the proper appreciation of any artwork or other cultural artifact is “an
experiential engagement of some kind with an instance of that work or ar-
tifact, whereby some or all of the properties bearing upon its appreciation

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 193
are made manifest to the receiver.”* The condition is necessary but not suffi-
cient because, arguably, artworks also have nonmanifest properties that are
artistically relevant and bear upon their appreciation. But let me first focus
on the necessity of experiential engagement.
The premise that the appreciation of a screenplay requires, at least, an
experiential engagement with an instance of the screenplay is, I hope, not
controversial. It is worth noting that Davies himself advances an argument
that could be seen as an interesting challenge. Suppose you accept the work/
text distinction drawn earlier and imagine two perceptually indistinguish-
able texts (two tokens of the same text-type), each of which is an instance
of a different work: Cervantes’s Don Quixote and Menard’s Don Quixote.
In Davies’s view, because the two text-tokens are identical, a text-token that
is a work-instance of Cervantes’s Quixote can be used for the appreciation
of Menard’s Quixote, and vice versa. As Davies puts its, “In either case, the
text is fully qualified to play the experiential role in the appreciation of the
work.”° To be clear, this is not to suggest that literary works are identifiable
with texts; on the contrary, the works themselves have distinct non-manifest,
contextual properties. But as long as the reader “locates” the properties of
the text in the context of Cervantes’s creation, any text-token that shares all
of the properties of the text of Cervantes’s Ouixote—including text-tokens
with intentional-historical links to Menard’s work—will suffice for an expe-
riential encounter with that work.° If this is right, then the premise may need
some fine-tuning, but the core idea underlying it remains unharmed. The ap-
preciation of a work requires, at least, some sort of experiential engagement
with an instance of the work (or, at least, an instance of some work, where
that instance shares all the same properties of the instance of the work one
is trying to appreciate).
In any case, I expect that if there are objections to this premise, they
will have to do with the claim that an experiential engagement with the
screenplay essentially involves reading it. Perhaps one might think that the
screenplay is accessible in virtue of viewing the film made from it. When
my partner and I watch the U.S. version of the television series The Killing
(AMC, 2011-) we frequently lament that although the lighting, set design,
and cinematography are great, the writing is hackneyed. How can we make
sense of such talk unless we retain the notion that we can access and appre-
ciate screenplays through films?
Marja-Riitta Koivumaki, who is one of the few scholars to have mounted
a sustained argument for the art-hood of the screenplay, has put this idea
more formally. On her view, “the screenplay is actualized for the viewer only
via cinematic performance.”” At the same time, she is rightly convinced that
the screenplay has an “aesthetic independence.”* She attempts to reconcile
these two claims by drawing a parallel between the screenplay’s relationship
to the film and the relationships that hold between performance plans and
performing arts. According to Koivumaki, “The screenplay, as a plan of any
performing art, is not meant to be received directly by the audience, but

194 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
indirectly via the performance itself.” Supposedly, films instantiate screen-
plays in the same manner that musical performances instantiate scores, and
so forth. And in each art form, the audience putatively has access to the plan
through the “performance.”
Although I agree with Koivumaki’s overall conclusion, I do not think
this is a convincing way to argue for it. As I tried to show in Chapter 6,
the screenplay’s relationship to the film is disanalagous to that which holds
between theatrical scripts and musical scores. In one sense, we can explain
the difference simply by noting that cinema is not a performing art—or,
at least, in our standard practices it is not a performing art in the same
sense that theater and music are performing arts. Our access to instances
of films, television shows, and the like is afforded by screenings, which are
mechanically or electronically generated from templates. A performance
is involved in the creation of some cinematic works, but not necessarily
all, and a performance is almost never involved into the generation of in-
stances of the work. On the other hand, the instantiation of a theatrical or
musical work necessarily requires a performance, but the creation of the
work does not.
In the cases of scripted theatrical works and notated musical works,
the work is created, without an instantiation, when the script or score is
complete. Thus, scripts and scores are work-determinative or work specify-
ing. Correctly following the instructions in the script or score will always
generate an instance of the same work. (However, the script and score are
not themselves instantiated in these performance-instances.) And this is the
other important way in which the screenplay/film relationship is disanala-
gous: screenplays are not work-specifying. The completion of a screenplay
does not entail the creation of a cinematic work, and correctly following
the instructions set out in a screenplay will not necessarily (if ever) result in
multiple instances of the same cinematic work. Furthermore, the screenplay
is not accessible through the film because the film does not make a sufficient
number of the screenplay’s constitutive properties manifest to the viewer.
Because the screenplay is an essentially verbal artifact, comprising descrip-
tions of images and sounds rather than actual images and sounds, film may
transmit very few of its properties (e.g., dialogue). And even when those
properties are so transmitted, this is a matter of contingency rather than
necessity. So, it seems implausible that screenplays are, in fact, accessed and
appreciated through film viewing.
A more plausible way to account for our tendency to talk about a film’s
screenplay or “writing” while watching and listening to it is this: Such talk
actually just refers to the film’s dialogue and its plotting. Although the film we
watch does not necessarily give us access to all and only the dialogue in the
screenplay, we speak this way because it is typically the case that most of a
film’s dialogue is taken from the screenplay. Likewise, although the narrative
structure of a film need not correspond to what is suggested in the screenplay,

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 195
enough films do follow the screenplay’s instructions for us to speak appre-
ciatively of narrative structure in terms of the film’s screenplay or its writing.
Thus, we tend to assume that when a film “falls apart” in the third act or
its climax is well orchestrated, the screenwriter(s) are to blame or praise. In
many cases, such remarks are accurate. But it is important to see that such
judgments are essentially educated guesses about what properties a screen-
play is likely to have given what the finished film looks and sounds like.
However, the film does not, in the end, provide a vehicle for us to ac-
cess or appreciate the screenplay properly. Of the many various properties
relevant to its appreciation, the film makes manifest to the viewer but a few
at the very most and, in some cases, none at all. What sorts of properties
are manifest to the reader solely in a screenplay? Perhaps needless to say,
much will vary from case to case. But for illustrative purposes, consider an
admittedly atypical example: the revised final screenplay for Alien (1979),
written by Walter Hill and David Giler, based on an earlier screenplay by
Dan O’Bannon. The screenplay begins like this:
INT. ENGINE ROOM
Empty, cavernous.
INT. ENGINE CUBICLE
Circular, jammed with instruments.
All of them idle.
Console chairs for two.
Empty.
INT. OILY CORRIDOR—“C” LEVEL
Long, dark.
Empty.
Turbos throbbing.
No other movement.
INT. CORRIDOR—“A ” LEVEL
Long, empty."”
Only an experiential encounter with the above text will make manifest
to the reader a number of properties that are obviously relevant to our
appreciation of the screenplay. At the very least, we ought to attend to
the repetition of certain words (“empty” and “long”), the sharp, staccato
rhythm of the prose, the sparseness of the language and the corresponding
sparseness of the space it describes. Overall, one wants to say that this is

196 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
an unusually and refreshingly poetic screenplay. Yet it would be impossible
to conclude this merely by watching Alien, without actually reading the
screenplay itself.
Assuming for a moment that such a conclusion is warranted, it illumi-
nates another previously raised point about the ontology of screenplays,
which bears upon their appreciation. Screenplays are not pure texts, but
rather are artifacts embedded in specific socio-historical contexts. To say
the Alien screenplay is refreshingly poetic is to say that it is so relative to
the practice of screenwriting in a particular time and place. Suppose that
in 2100, it is standard practice in Hollywood to write screenplays in haiku
form. If a screenplay that shared the same text as the Alien screenplay were
written in that context, it would not be refreshingly poetic but, perhaps,
poorly executed or dull. The point, argued at length in Chapter 8, is that not
all of the properties relevant to our appreciation of screenplays are manifest
in their texts; some are relational properties that no textual investigation
will descry. As David Davies puts it,
the generative activities through which artworks come into existence,
while they frequently issue in objects, or instances of structure-types,
which possess certain “manifest” properties, also serve to confer upon
those entities certain “nonmanifest” properties or values that seem cen-
tral to the appreciation of the resulting works."!
So although a necessary condition for the appreciation of a screenplay is an
experiential engagement with a text, such an engagement is not sufficient.
The appreciation of a screenplay further requires us to understand its tex-
tual properties as the results of an intentional act of creation in the context
of screenwriting practice at a particular moment in history.
WAYS OF APPRECIATING SCREENPLAYS
Based on what I have said so far, it may sound as if there is no room in my
account for the rather common critical practice of comparing screenplays
with finished films. On the contrary, I think there is a place for this practice
in the appreciation of screenplays, but we need to take care here. Again,
it is illuminating to draw a contrast with the relationship between theatrical
scripts and theatrical performances. Because screenplays are not work-
specifying the goals of our comparisons of screenplays with films and the-
atrical scripts with performances are somewhat different. In the latter case,
we are interested in how an abstract work for performance, the constitutive
properties of which are prescribed by the script, is realized on the stage. So,
we are interested in what the script requires and permits for instances of the
work it specifies. In the former case, on the contrary, we are interested in
how the screenplay was used in the production process, whether or not its

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 197
instructions were followed, and, if so, how. Thus, we are interested in what
the screenplay suggests the film ought to be like and how the film does or
does not conform to those suggestions. This distinction between script and
screenplay may appear to be slight, but, as I have argued, it marks a signifi-
cant ontological difference. Despite this, there are fundamental similarities
between theatrical script and screenplay in the context of appreciation. In
both cases, we are interested in how verbal instructions are creatively inter-
preted in a distinct artistic context.
Furthermore, then, part of the appreciation of a screenplay usually in-
volves attending to what a screenplay suggests about what a film should be
like and how it does so.'* Consider, for example, a scene near the end of the
screenplay for White Heat (1949):
TOP OF HORTONSPHERE NIGHT
As Cody reaches it he realizes, dimly at first, that he has reached a dead
end. There is no way down but the ladder he climbed. Voices off-screen,
drawing closer. Weaving slightly, aware now he has chosen his own
death-trap, he stands astride top of Hortonsphere, which is like the
globe itself. He stands there alone, naked and unprotected, challenging
the world in its madness.!%
In this passage, our interest is not merely in the text itself, but in what sug-
gestions are made for how the scene is to be staged in order to create a visual
image with thematic significance. To be specific, the image of Cody atop the
globe is clearly intended to evoke a kind of tragic irony. Crafted as it is in the
screenplay, the image is arguably heavyhanded and trite. However, we can
imagine that if the instructions in the passage were followed in production,
the result might well be a subtle, affective visual metaphor. (And, arguably,
this is what White Heat gives us.) Thus, although we cannot appreciate the
White Heat screenplay by watching White Heat, neither can we fully ap-
preciate it by reading it without regard for its intended role in the creation
of a film. And this is generally true of screenplays that are intended to play
a role in film production.
A more general point, for which I argued in Part I, is that screenplays
have certain intentional properties essentially. Intentional properties, as a
class of relational properties, figure centrally into our appreciative practices.
Although I have been at pains to show that a screenplay is ontologically and
aesthetically autonomous from any particular film, it is nevertheless typically
the case that a screenplay is written with the intention that it be used in the
creation of some film. In such cases, this sort of intention ought to inflect
our reading of the screenplay for the sorts of reasons illustrated by the White
Heat example. Arguably, the writers pay relatively little attention to the way
language is used to create the image of Cody on top of the world because
their primary intention is for the image to be realized in another medium.

198 <A Philosophy of the Screenplay
I suspect this example generalizes to many passages in countless screenplays.
The point is not that the screenplay is a lesser form of literature for this, but
that it is a distinct form of literature—the works of which are created with
distinct intentions from the intentions with which, say, poetry is created.
As I stressed in Part I, not all screenplays are intended for use in pro-
duction and this fact similarly bears upon their appreciation. To approach
a fan-fiction screenplay in just the same way as a Golden-Age Hollywood
continuity script is to misunderstand, in some fundamental way, what the
writer(s) of the former are trying to do. To be sure, the writers of the fan-
fiction screenplay intend to connect their work to the same practice of which
the Hollywood screenplay is a part, and they intend their work to be similar
in some relevant ways. But they do not intend, for example, that their script
ensures there is sufficient coverage of every scene or that there is tight conti-
nuity from scene to scene, and it would be a mistake to criticize it on these
grounds. Rather, a general goal of the fan-fiction screenplay seems to be to
use conventional screenplay form to relay a narrative that offers readers a
rich imaginative experience by engendering vivid mental imagery.
Consider, for example, the first scene of an episode of the virtual series,
Walker.
Black Screen.
The North Sea.
We HEAR the sounds of WIND, HAMMERING RAIN and
CRASHING WAVES.
FADE IN:
EXT. OCEAN—NIGHT
Thunder rumbles and rain crashes down into the sea. The waves rise
and chop. A deadly, terrifying storm.
In the distance we can make out a shape—which is then illuminated
by a single spot of light.
CLOSER IN, we get a better look—the light burns at the top of an
OIL RIG. A sign on one side bears the legend: NORTH SEA OIL."
Perhaps in such cases of fan-fiction writing, we should expect there to be
closer attention to language, since here the screenplay is not a set of instruc-
tions for the creation of another work, but rather the only work. Further,
we might expect that the writers of screenplays that are not for production
approach the creation of images differently, since they need not worry about
suggesting what the images might look like captured on film, but only how
the reader might conjure them in her mind. In any case, these are provisional
hypotheses and further study of fan-fiction screenplay writing could refine
them, but the point is simply that the differences in intentional properties

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 199
between screenplays-for-production and screenplays-not-for-production
have consequences for our appreciative practices.
It is worth noting in passing that reflection upon the intentional proper-
ties of screenplays can help us to recognize and avoid what appears to be
one inappropriate approach to the comparison of screenplays and films. One
might be tempted to think that if screenplays are necessarily ontologically
autonomous works, then every film made from a screenplay is therefore an
adaptation of it. However, I think that approaching films as adaptations of
screenplays would be misguided. Screenplays do not seem to be the kinds of
things that admit of filmic adaptation because of their particular intentional
properties. More specifically, it would not make sense to say that a cinematic
adaptation was made from a screenplay that was intended for production or
that such a screenplay was adapted to film. The problem is that the concept
of adaptation seems to essentially involve at least some sort of use of a work
in a way not originally intended by the work’s creator. (Note that this charac-
terization does allow for instances of self-adaptation.) But most screenplays
are intended to be used in film production—in the creation of a distinct work.
Screenplays that are not intended for production complicate matters. It is
tempting to suspect that if a film were made from a screenplay that was in-
tended not to be used for production, but solely to be read, we would have a
genuine case of cinematic adaptation. To my knowledge, no such case exists,
but I leave it to the reader to test her intuitions about hypothetical examples.
The central point I want to draw from this discussion of the intentional
properties—and relational properties, more broadly—of screenplays is that
to read a screenplay as an autonomous literary work is not, as some critics
suggest, to tear it from the context in which it was written. On the contrary,
my argument is that the proper appreciation of a screenplay necessarily in-
volves attending not simply to the text itself, but also to certain facts about
the context in which it was written—facts about, for example, the particular
screenwriting practice and the moment in that practice’s history in which the
screenplay is embedded, as well as the categorical intentions with which
the screenplay was written. As I argued in Chapter 8, such facts determine the
relational properties of a given screenplay and, thus, its aesthetic properties.
The appreciation of a screenplay requires some contextual knowledge (or, at
least, some plausible hypotheses) because otherwise one does not even know
how to approach the screenplay, how to go about trying to appreciate it.
It bears emphasis that attention to the categorical intentions with which a
screenplay is written involves taking stock of the fact that a given screenplay
has (or has not) been created to serve as a set of instructions for shooting a
film of particular kind. Let me attempt to head off some anticipated objec-
tions from screen studies scholars by clarifying what I mean by “categorical
intentions.” My use of the term follows a distinction made by Jerrold Levin-
son. In his account, the creation of art involves two distinct sorts of inten-
tions: intentions about work-meaning (semantic intentions) and intentions
about what kind of thing to create and how that thing is to be approached
(categorical intentions). In his words:

200 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
Categorical intentions involve the maker’s framing and position of his
product vis-a-vis his projected audience; they involve the maker’s con-
ception of what he has produced and what it is for, on a rather basic
level; they govern not what a work is to mean but how it is to be funda-
mentally conceived or approached.’
The point here is that my claim is only about the relevance of categorical in-
tentions for the appreciation of screenplays. These are the sorts of intentions
that, I argued in Chapter 2, actually determine the sort of thing an artisan
or artist creates (assuming they are successfully realized). In my view, then,
part of appreciating a screenplay is reading it with the knowledge that, say,
it is intended to be used for shooting a Hollywood film, or used for shoot-
ing an experimental film, or only to be read. For, again, the facts about such
intentions bear upon the aesthetic properties that the screenplay has. In this
sense, to read the screenplay as a literary work is to read it with a consider-
ation of its context in film production.
Nevertheless, this is not the sort of consideration of production context
that some critics seem to think is necessary. Steven Maras, for example,
writes, “One general problem with the attempt to see the screenplay as a
form of literature is that it tends to take the script out of its production
context, restrict this intermediality and treat it as an autonomous work of
art.”'® Maras’s position seems to be that a proper reading of the screenplay
should consider its role in the production of a specific film and its status as
“intermedial” relative to that finished film. Furthermore, the implication
here is that reading the screenplay this way is incompatible with reading it
as an autonomous work of literature.
The first point to make in response to Maras is this: To say that the screen-
play is an autonomous work is merely to make a claim about its ontology—
not to make a claim about how it is to be read vis-a-vis its production context.
True, my motivation for arguing that the screenplay is ontologically autono-
mous is partly to thwart objections to the notion that it can be a work of art
in its own right. But neither does the claim that some screenplays are art-
works entail any particular position about how they should be read vis-a-vis
their production contexts.
However, there is an irony to Maras’s worry about reading the screenplay
divorced from its specific production context: To read a screenplay as an
“intermedial” work—as one might do as part of a kind of industrial or his-
torical analysis—is to read it in a way that was not intended by its writer(s).
For being able to study the screenplay as “intermedial” requires one to
have some experience or knowledge of a finished film in virtue of which the
screenplay can be regarded as such. But, obviously, nobody who is using a
screenplay in the production of a film can situate it as “intermedial,” and
the writer does not intend it to be read this way if she actually intends it for
production. The screenplay is not “intermedial” until there is some finished
film made from it. This is not to deny there is a place for reading screenplays

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 201
as part of historical or industrial research. But it is worth noting that oddity
of the suggestion that a screenplay that has been used in production should
(usually? always?) be read as “intermedial”—in a way it was not originally
intended to be read.
Furthermore, I want to challenge the idea, implicit in Maras’s claim, that
reading a screenplay qua literary work and qua production tool are mutually
exclusive. Suppose for a moment that to read a text as a work of literature is,
basically, to read it as an artifact, intentionally designed to realize a number
of aesthetically relevant features whose reception is worthwhile for its own
sake—features such as form and structure, character development, theme,
highly wrought language used to create images, cue imaginative engagement,
and arouse emotion, and so forth.'” It seems to me that if this rough-and-
ready characterization of literary appreciation is even close to accurate, then
one’s reading (and successful use) of a screenplay in the production process
actually requires one to read it as a literary work in many, if not all, cases.
Certainly this is true in cases where the screenplay, as a whole, is more
an evocation of a mood or idea than a set of specific instructions. Recall
Su Friedrich’s screenplay for But No One (1982), which I mentioned in
Chapter 2:
Dark sky
Fat boy stands on a ledge
Women stand grief-stricken by the canal
Babies of all races float by
In colorful clothes
All dead or dying
Little mouths crying above the water
The must grab
Grab the live ones
He
She
He can’t won’t doesn’t
Grab the live ones
But
No more float by'®
To use this screenplay—or any similarly poetic screenplay—in the process of
film production is necessarily to read the screenplay as a literary work. Of
course, in this particular case, Friedrich shot her own screenplay and may
have known from the start how she was going to cinematically interpret
certain elements. But this does not detract from the broader point that any
cinematic interpretation of the screenplay would require attention to, say,
the image of floating babies, “All dead or dying,” the rhythm created by the
form, and the repetition of the line, “Grab the live ones.” One could hardly
shoot the screenplay without reading it as a literary work.

202 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
But I think that upon reflection, it is clear that the same is true in many
cases. Imagine, for example, you were hired as the director of photography
for The Tree of Life (2011). You need to come up with a general idea of how
the screenplay should be filmed from a cinematographer’s point of view.
You might go about your business this way. First, you conceive of an over-
all look and feel of the images, which will be determined by more specific
choices about film stocks, camera lenses, framings, camera movements, and
lighting. Then, you break the script down and make local decisions about
these features within specific scenes. But the screenplay Terrence Malick has
sent you is, like most contemporary screenplays, bereft of specific instruc-
tions with regard to these matters. Imagine having to plan shooting the fol-
lowing scene near the end of the screenplay:
SCENES HIS SPIRIT SEES—CRANE AND ARIAL SHOTS
Fields, forests, tall mountains: the world is restored
to its original beauty. His soul has emerged from
childhood. He soars like a god.
His has been a journey into light, into love; out of
error into truth, from grief to consolation, out of
death to the shores of a new, unending life.
The eternal is not one thing, and he another. He grows
from it like a tree from the earth.'”
How to light and shoot this? Well, of course, you want to brainstorm ideas
with the writer/director, but you have to start somewhere. It seems to me
that in this hypothetical case—and in many real ones—what is required is
reading of the screenplay that attends to things such as how its language
creates a sense of tone, how its structure suggests a purpose or point, how
its narration focalizes and story and, perhaps, encourages the adoption of a
particular point of view. In short, what is needed is to read the screenplay in
the same relevant ways one would read any work of literary fiction.
Consider a specific example, this time from the point of view of an actor.
This time, imagine you are Dustin Hoffman preparing for your role as Ben
in The Graduate (1967). Imagine further that shooting is taking place out
of order—as it often does on film sets—and the first scene you shoot is at
the end of the first act. After previously rebuffing Mrs. Robinson’s advances,
Ben invites her to a hotel room with the intention of sleeping with her. Mrs.
Robinson has just arrived and suggested that Ben watch her undress. Here
is just a brief portion of the scene in the screenplay:
MRS. ROBINSON
Will you bring me a hanger?

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 203
BEN
What?
MRS. ROBINSON
A hanger.
Ben opens the closet door.
BEN
Oh—yes. Wood?
MRS. ROBINSON
What?
BEN
Wood or wire? They have both.?°
As in the previous example, it seems clear that before you arrive on set or
even meet with the director, you would have to start preparing the scene by
carefully reading the entire screenplay. To play the part of Ben, the sorts of
features to which you would need to attend would at least include narrative
structure, the narrative’s overall theme or point, the character arcs of Ben
and the other main characters, and the tone created by the language used in
the script. Furthermore, it is evident that to successfully play this particular
scene (and many others like it), you would need to read the dialogue and
scene action with careful attention to subtext. Again, it seems that, from
the point of view of a member of the production team, the sort of reading
of the screenplay that is required to actually make a film is tantamount to
a reading of the screenplay as a literary work. As an actor, one has to read
the screenplay as a work that has been intentionally designed to reward at-
tention to the aforementioned features, which are surely common elements
of most literary narratives. So, there are good reasons to think that reading
a screenplay in a production context or as a production tool just is, in many
cases, reading a screenplay as a literary work.
Now consider the reverse scenario, in which one reads a produced screen-
play “purely as a literary work” in an office, a classroom, a library, or an
easy chair, with no knowledge whatsoever of how it was actually used in
the production process. It is not entirely clear what the problems with this
sort of reading are supposed to be. In the context of theater, it is part of
our standard appreciative practices to read theatrical scripts such as Peter
Shaffer’s Equus in this sort of way and our practices seem no worse off for
it. And it is worth remembering that the script Equus specifies a theatrical
work, Equus, in contrast to the screenplay, which specifies no particular cin-
ematic work. So, it is not clear why we should not read a screenplay without
a particular film in mind. If the screenplay were shot again, it would result
in a different film—and perhaps one sort of pleasure the screenplay affords
involves the imaging what a different film made from it would be like.

204 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
It may be true that sometimes, as a matter of contingent fact, a particu-
lar screenplay is inextricably intertwined with a particular film production.
Arguably, this is the case when the screenplay is written on set, according to
specific production constraints (for example, sick cast members, inclement
weather, a budget shortage, and so on). For example, the final draft (and
published version) of the screenplay for Sweet Smell of Success (1957) has
some of the properties it does because Clifford Odets was writing, in the
back of a van, pages for scenes that had to be shot the same night. In such
instances, we may want to say that a screenplay is inseparable from the
production of a particular film.
This seems right, but it does not constitute an objection to my argument
about the ontological relationship between screenplay and cinematic work.
Steven Maras claims that there is “a significant issue when considering the
script as literature . . . which is that the existence of the script is bound to the
cinematic medium, and also to the production of films.”?! But this is mani-
festly false. It is evident that there are numerous scripts—unmade scripts,
spec scripts, fan-fiction scripts, student scripts—that are not involved in the
production of films, let alone bound to the cinematic medium. Pace Maras,
the fact that, as a matter of contingency, some screenplays are essentially
involved in the production processes of specific films entails no ontological
conclusions about the nature of the screenplay. But it is still not clear why
even in these sorts of cases Maras seems to have in mind there is a prob-
lem with reading the script, in complete ignorance of its specific production
context, as a work of literature. (And, in any case, how much knowledge of
the production context would be required for a “proper” reading?) What
exactly is the objection?
For one thing, the objection cannot be that such readings ignore the in-
tentions of the writer(s) about how the screenplay should be approached.
I understand Maras’s suggestion to be that one must read a produced screen-
play as an object that is part of the history of a particular film production.
But if 1a m wrong, and the idea is merely that one needs to read a produced
screenplay with the knowledge that it was created for the general purpose
of shooting a film, then we have no disagreement. For, as I have tried to em-
phasize, the proper appreciation of a screenplay—or any artwork, for that
matter—must be attuned to the categorical intentions of the writer or artist.
SCREENPLAY READING AND MENTAL IMAGING
If what I have argued up to this point is roughly accurate, then broadly
speaking, appreciating a screenplay as a work of literature involves reading
it as an intentionally designed artifact, simultaneously situated in the his-
tory of dramatic literary practice as a whole, the practice of screenwriting
(broadly construed), and, perhaps, a more specific tradition of screenwrit-
ing (e.g., Hollywood screenwriting, fan-fiction screenwriting, avant-garde

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 205
screenwriting). Furthermore, as I suggested in Chapter 7, appreciating a
screenplay qua work of literature is akin to appreciating other, familiar
works of literature such as poems and novels inasmuch as it is likely to in-
volve (depending on the particular screenplay) attention to how words are
used to create aesthetically relevant features (e.g., metaphor) worthwhile
reading for their own sake, provide substantive cognitive value, afford an
engaging imaginative experience, and so forth. But one may wonder if there
is not some more specific kind of appreciative engagement that is particular
to the screenplay. In other words, does the appreciation of screenplays as
literary works entail a particular kind of reading?
I want to explore what seems to be at least a characteristic, if not essen-
tial, strategy or manner of reading screenplays that has developed histori-
cally alongside screenwriting practice. If screenplays have historically been
written for the purpose of shooting films (even if they do not necessarily
have this intended function), then it seems reasonable to suppose that many
screenplays invite their readers to visualize how the events contained therein
might look on film. Of course, most of the moving image artworks (films
and otherwise) we ordinarily encounter comprise not just moving images,
but also sounds. And most screenplays suggest what a film should sound
like—most notably in terms of dialogue, but also in terms of sound effects—
as well what a film ought to look like. So, to be more precise, we ought to
say that screenplays often cue readers to conjure mental imagery—usually
visual and auditory imagery, but also, on occasion, olfactory imagery, haptic
imagery, and perhaps other sorts of mental imagery, as well.
That is, at least most screenplays afford the sort of quasiperceptual experi-
ence to which we colloquially refer as “seeing with the mind’s eye,” “having
a mental picture,” “visualizing,” or the like. I hasten to add that it is un-
likely that all screenplays are intended to offer their readers such experiences;
surely some merely ask the reader to follow the instructions specified. Con-
ceivably, one could follow the instructions of an extremely minimal screen-
play and shoot a film without ever visualizing what the finished product (or
parts thereof) ought to look like. And while it may seem unlikely that there
could be a literary work that afforded no mental imaging whatsoever, I am
not quite prepared to suggest that the appreciation of a screenplay qua liter-
ary work necessarily involves imaging. If for no other reason, this is because
I doubt we want to deny that people who claim not to conjure any mental
imagery whatsoever are able to appreciate screenplays as literature.
I also doubt that a screenplay’s capacity to cue mental imaging is enough
to make it a work of literature or that conjuring mental imagery is all that
is involved in appreciating a screenplay qua literary work. If a gaffer reads a
portion of a screenplay to conjure a precise image of how light hits a char-
acter’s face at a particular moment, she is not necessarily engaged in the ap-
preciation of the screenplay as literature. As I have emphasized, there must
at least be some further necessary conditions that one situate the screenplay
in the right sort of contexts.

206  A Philosophy of the Screenplay
|     | that the conjuring of mental  |     | imagery is a neces-  |     |
| --- | ----------------------------- | --- | -------------------- | --- |
So my claim is neither
is it that
sary part of the appreciation of screenplays as literary works, nor
the conjuring of mental imagery while reading is sufficient for a proper ap-
|                             |               |         | It is, rather,  that  | for reasons  |
| --------------------------- | ------------- | ------- | --------------------- | ------------ |
| preciation  of screenplays  | as  literary  | works.  |                       |              |
having to do with the history of screenwriting practice and the categorical
intentions of screenwriters, screenplays typically encourage the conjuring of
mental imagery and readers typically approach screenplays with the expec-
tation of being cued to engage in imaging. Moreover,  in the loosely defined
community or institution of screenwriting and screenplay-reading,
it is gen-
erally accepted that conjuring mental imagery is a central part of reading a
screenplay.”
Now part of the reason  it seems  plausible that mental  imaging is, char-
acteristically,  centrally  involved  in the appreciation  of screenplays  qua lit-
erature  is that conjuring mental  imagery seems  to be an  important part of
our appreciation of more familiar sorts of literary works such as poems and
novels. Although the cueing and experience of mental imagery have received
relatively little attention  from literary critics,  I doubt  it is controversial  to
say that, in many cases, mental imagery constitutes an important part of the
experience  literary works  are  designed  to elicit.>  Given  this fact, it seems
that the interesting question about screenplay reading is not whether it in-
volves mental imagery, but rather what, exactly, the nature of screenplay-led
imaging is and whether it is the same  in kind as the mental  imaging facili-
tated by other sorts of literature. Is there a distinctive sort of mental imagery
engendered by screenplays?
Perhaps what seems  to make the screenplay special is that it facilitates  a
particular imagery experience that is somehow like the experience of movie
watching.  According  to a popular view,  reading a well-written  screenplay
gives rise to the experience of “seeing” a movie in the mind’s eye. This idea,
in one form or another, is widely held by a both practitioners and theorists.
For example, Anthony Friedmann, the author of Writing for Visual Media,
claims:  “If you  are  a trained  director  or  editor  who  knows  how  to  read
a script,  you  can  visualize  in your  mind’s  eye what  is intended,  just as  a
musician  can  hear in his mind’s  ear  what  the music  should  sound  like.”?4
Friedmann’s  slide between  talking about the quasiperceptual  experience of
visualizing and the actual perceptual experience of hearing is telling. One’s
| experience  of mental  | imagery can  | be so     |             |               |
| ---------------------- | ------------ | --------- | ----------- | ------------- |
|                        |              | vivid—so  | apparently  | like percep-  |
tual experience—that one may slip into talking about hearing inner sounds
| or  seeing inner  | pictures.  Of course,  | Friedmann  |                            |      |
| ----------------- | ---------------------- | ---------- | -------------------------- | ---- |
|                   |                        |            | is not,  in this context,  | at-  |
tempting to offer a nuanced theoretical account
of our mental activity dur-
ing screenplay  reading,  but is simply  harnessing  a bit of folk  psychology
| to  make a practical  | point  about  |                  |                  |          |
| --------------------- | ------------- | ---------------- | ---------------- | -------- |
