screenplays.
| However,  | some  scholars  and  | theoretically  | minded         |       |
| --------- | -------------------- | -------------- | -------------- | ----- |
|           |                      |                | practitioners  | have  |
taken seriously the notion of seeing a movie in the mind’s eye as a theoreti-
cal account of our experience of screenplay reading. Pier Paolo Pasolini, for

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 207
example, suggests in an oft-cited essay, “the characteristic technique [of the
screenplay] is a special and canonical request for collaboration from the
reader to see the kineme [visual signs] in the grapheme [written signs], above
all, and thus to think in images, reconstructing in his own head the film to
which the screenplay alludes as a potential work.” Again, there is an in-
teresting slippage between talking about actually seeing things and thinking
in images, but, in any case, Pasolini seems to be suggesting that reading a
screenplay involves an experience that is something similar to constructing
and seeing a movie in our mind’s eye.
More recently, Steven Maras has offered the most sustained scholarly
discussion of screenplay-reading and mental imaging, so I will quote him at
length here:
The unique aspect of the script as screenplay is the way it functions as
an audio-imaging machine, a playback device or registration device spe-
cific to the page. Boyle observes that “reading a script is a bit like view-
ing a film. The pages should be turned constantly and uniformly the way
the film is projected smoothly without ever turning back” (1983: v).
As such the screenplay is a fascinating invention, performing a function
that is different to other script forms . . . the screenplay facilitates a per-
sonal preview of the film to come, a process McKee calls ‘putting a film
in the reader’s head’ (1997: 94).°
It seems to me that there is a bit of ambiguity here; it is not entirely clear
in which parts of the passage Maras is just speaking metaphorically. To be
fair, he writes with more circumspection a bit later in his discussion: “The
idea of a reader seeing and hearing a film ‘on the screen of the mind’s eye’,
in a kind of immersive experience, is an important aspect of the screenplay.
But one wonders if the claims made about this aspect of the screenplay can
be overstated.””” Indeed, this is putting things mildly. One might justifiably
wonder if claims about watching movies in the mind’s eye are not deeply
confused—perhaps even nonsensical. How, exactly, could someone have a
film in her head?
Yet, we ought to take pause because the prevalence of this sort of talk
in our creative and critical practices suggests that there is an intuitive pull
to idea of screenplay-reading affording an experience that at least feels like
viewing some sort of internal movie. And this, I imagine, is why Pasolini
and Maras take the notion of seeing a movie in the mind’s eye seriously as
a theoretical idea—not because they actually believe there are miniature
films in our heads. However, the nature of the activity they describe and
its accuracy as a theory of what we experience when reading a screenplay
remain far from clear. In what follows, I give three reasons we should, in
our theorization of the screenplay if not in common parlance, dispense with
the idea of seeing movies in the mind’s eye: (1) our mental images are quite
unlike movies in a number of important ways; (2) the experience of having

208 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
a mental image differs significantly from the experience of perception; and
(3) screenplays underdetermine the constitutive properties of movies. I
should immediately add, however, that my arguments do not threaten
the plausible idea that an important part of the experience of reading a
screenplay involves visualization or, more broadly, the conjuring of mental
imagery—an idea, that we should indeed retain.
To begin, we ought to distinguish between the idea that reading a screen-
play may facilitate a quasiperceptual experience similar in some respects
to the experience of viewing a film and the idea that reading a screenplay
engenders the construction of quasi(moving) pictures (and sounds) in the
head. As the philosopher Nigel Thomas notes, “far too many discussions of
visual mental imagery fail to draw a clear distinction between the conten-
tion that people have quasivisual experiences and the contention that such
experiences are to be explained by the presence of representations, in the
mind or brain, that are in some sense picture-like.”** Now, in many ways,
the philosophical debate about the nature of mental imagery touches on is-
sues beyond our purview at present. However, it is important to establish at
the outset that if the claim that reading a screenplay facilitates the viewing
of a movie in the mind’s eye is to even be coherent, it must be taken to mean
that the reading affords an experience that is in some ways like viewing a
movie.
The alternative possibility, that reading a screenplay gives rise to mental
representations that are “movielike,” faces many of the same criticisms that
have been lodged against the picture theory of mental imagery. Developed
and defended most notably by psychologist Stephen M. Kosslyn and his
research associates, the picture theory holds that “visual mental images can
be characterized as short-term memory representations of visual events that
give rise to the experience of ‘seeing with the mind’s eye.’”?? More spe-
cifically, according to the picture theory, these representations are depictive
in nature. The reasons for this are complex and, fortunately for me, not
relevant here, but the motivating idea is, in Kosslyn’s words, that “visual
mental images are formed by using stored information to reconstruct spatial
patterns in topographically organized cortical areas [of the brain].”°° Thus,
although Kosslyn and his associates acknowledge that “mental images are
fundamentally different from pictures in some ways,” they maintain that the
properties of images are “quasipictorial.”*!
Kosslyn and his associates argue that a number of the objections to the
picture theory attack a straw man—to wit, the idea that mental images are
pictures in the brain. Their reply is that “no researcher in the field would
seriously argue that visual mental images are pictures; pictures are concrete
objects that exist in the world, whereas mental images are internal represen-
tations.”* *S imilarly, Iw ould be surprised if any philosopher of film or screen
studies theorist believed that the imagery cued by reading a screenplay was
properly described as a “movie in the head,” as screenwriting “guru” Rob-
ert McKee puts it. And in any case, as far as I can tell, none of the talk about

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 209
screenplay reading as allowing one to see a movie in the mind’s eye involves
a claim about the nature of the imagery involved. Rather, such talk seems to
claim that the experience of imaging during screenplay reading is somehow
like seeing a movie in the mind’s eye. For this reason, I will note only one
difference between mental images and movies—one that looks forward to
the more substantive points I want to make about the experiential differ-
ences between imaging and movie viewing.
Of the many differences between images and percepts, here is one that
both highlights the contrasts between screenplay-cued imagery and actual
movies and undermines the idea that such imagery can be characterized as
quasimovies, inner movies, or the like. The contents of mental images bear
a causal relationship to the mental actions of an agent; the contents of per-
cepts do not. Further, the contents of mental images are typically intention-
ally determined; the contents of one’s percepts, however, are never affected
by one’s intentions.**As Wittgenstein observes, “The concept of imagining is
rather like one of doing than receiving. Imagining might be called a creative
act.”** For this reason, he notes, “We are not surprised by these pictures
[that is, mental images], saying ‘Look!’ ”* Similarly, Sartre writes, “No mat-
ter how long I may look at an image, I shall never find anything in it but
what was put there. It is in this fact that we find the distinction between
an image and a perception.”** So, for example, one might be surprised to
see a monster burst out of John Hurt’s chest in Alien (1979), but one could
not be surprised by the presence of a monster in a visual mental image one
conjured while reading a screenplay.
This contrast between image and percept points to a couple of significant
experiential differences between seeing a movie in one’s mind’s eye and seeing
an actual movie. First, the arousal of emotions such as surprise, shock, and
suspense—surely an important part of much movie viewing—has no equiva-
lent in the context of imaging.*” To be sure, one can feel such emotions while
reading a screenplay, but it is important to be clear that those emotions are
elicited by the screenplay itself and not by the imagery conjured while read-
ing. The arousal of at least some feelings of surprise and suspense appears
so fundamental to our experience of narrative film (for until the film is over,
some suspense is always created by the uncertainty of how things will turn
out)?* that our imagery’s lack of equivalent experience might alone be reason
enough to eschew comparisons of imaging and movie watching altogether.
Yet there is, underlying this difference between the experience of imaging
and movie viewing, an even more fundamental contrast. Again, Wittgen-
stein is helpful here. As he puts it, “When we form an image of something
we are not observing.”*? One way to grasp this point is to note that the
concept of observation conditions has no applicability in the context of
mental imagery.*® The conditions under which one watches a movie may
range from poor to ideal and, moreover, it makes sense to try to improve
those conditions if one has trouble perceiving the movie. Suppose the cin-
ema manager has forgotten to dim the lights or one’s seat is too far from the

210 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
speakers. Lowering the lights or moving one’s seat will allow one to better
observe the movie. In contrast, it makes little sense to speak of observation
conditions at all in the context of mental imagery. One could hardly raise or
lower the lights on one’s visual mental image or adjust the volume of one’s
auditory mental image. For one does not view or hear a mental image as
one views or hears a movie. Thus, it is a stretch to say, as Maras does, that
the screenplay functions as an “audio-imaging machine, a playback device”
not only because it plays nothing back, but also because it generates neither
sounds nor pictures to be observed.
Indeed, it is unclear how mental images could be observed. In the first
place, there is, as M. R. Bennett and P.M.S. Hacker point out, a contrast
with regard to our use of sense organs. We use our eyes to perceive pictures
and our ears to perceive sounds, but there is no organ of perception of
mental images. “Imaging,” argues Gilbert Ryle, ““ is not having shadowy
pictures before some shadow-organ called ‘the mind’s eye.’”*' One might
argue that we straightforwardly view and hear our visual and auditory men-
tal images because we supposedly see and hear not with our eyes and ears
but with our brains.*? However, this is an unconvincing line of reasoning,
for at least two reasons. First, we would do well here to recall Wittgenstein’s
remark, “Only of a human being and what resembles (behaves like) a living
human being can one say: it has sensations; it sees, is blind; hears, is deaf; is
conscious or unconscious.”** For seeing is a behavior that requires agency;
it is not something a brain can do. Furthermore, as J. Kevin O’Regan and
Alva Noe argue, seeing “necessarily involves particular forms of action and
reaction on the part of the visual apparatus and the environment . . . speci-
fying the brain state is not sufficient to determine the sensory experience,
because we need to know how the visual apparatus and the environment are
interacting. There can therefore be no one-to-one correspondence between
visual experience and neural activations.”**
But even if it were granted, for the sake of argument, that our eyes are not
necessary for seeing (and ears for hearing), this still does not explain how we
could possibly view and hear mental images (or, in this context, our mental
movies). Although some research suggests that the same neural mechanisms
underlie visual perception and visual mental imagery, there are recent stud-
ies that have called this conclusion into question.** However, even if visual
perception and visual mental imagery do involve the same neural structures
and processes, visual mental images still cannot be properly said to be seen
because they are mind-dependent; they do not exist in objective reality wait-
ing for us to perceive them. Mental images are things we create rather than
things that we observe. And even if the proponent of visible mental images
rejects these claims, the burden of proof is still on her to show how we could
be said to be seeing or hearing such inner images (on the brain) without
positing the existence of an inner intelligent agent—a homunculus—who
perceives and makes sense of them.*¢
If these points are roughly accurate, then we court conceptual confu-
sion when we speak of viewing, watching, seeing, or hearing movies in the

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 211
mind’s eye. You can, as Friedmann claims, “visualize in your mind’s eye
what is intended [by a screenplay],” but this is not at all “just as a musician
can hear in his mind’s ear what the music should sound like.” For neither
are there sounds inside the musician’s head, nor can one hear one’s audi-
tory images. As Ryle writes, “When I fancy I am hearing a very loud noise,
I am not really hearing either a loud or faint noise; I am not having a mild
auditory sensation, as I am not having an auditory sensation at all, though
Tam fancying that I am having an intense one.”*” So, too, it is with the im-
aging afforded by screenplay reading; one may fancy one is having visual
and auditory sensations, but no actual perceptual experience is undergone
as a result. In the interest of conceptual clarity, we would do well to talk
about screenplays in terms of their capacity to facilitate visualization or
imaging.
So far I have identified two reasons for being wary about talk of mental
movie viewing: our mental images are quite unlike movies and the expe-
rience of imaging differs significantly from the experience of perception.
There is one more reason to be wary of the idea that screenplay reading
facilitates movie viewing in the minds eye, and it can be drawn out by recall-
ing the disanalogy between musical scores and screenplays. The surface ap-
peal of comparing, as Friedmann does, the mental imaging that occurs while
reading a screenplay and while reading a score derives from the fact that the
score is constitutive of a musical work and makes the work available to a
sufficiently informed reader. Thus, a particularly talented reader who was
entirely unfamiliar with a given musical work could, in principle, simultane-
ously read and interpret the score, and imagine (through the conjuring of
mental auditory imagery) what the corresponding work would sound like in
performance. In this regard, if two readers (first-time readers or otherwise)
of the same score have mental auditory imagery at all, their imagings are, of
necessity, of the same work (or parts thereof), even if their imagery differs
somewhat. And it makes sense to talk about these readers both imaging a
musical work or the musical work.
However, as I have been arguing, the screenplay is not constitutive of a
cinematic work and makes no cinematic work available to its reader. There-
fore, when two readers of the same unfilmed screenplay conjure imagery
(visual and auditory), they are actually visualizing different potential mov-
ies. This is also true in cases where a film has been made from the screen-
play, but readers have not seen it. (On the other hand, screenplay readers
may visualize the same film if, as a matter of fact, a film has been made
from the screenplay and their readings trigger the visualization of the same
finished film or a part thereof. But here we want to say that the screenplay
is not actually constitutive of their visualizations. Rather, the screenplay
reminds them of images or sounds in the film, which is responsible for their
visualizations.)
So, there are two ways in which the nature of the screenplay presents
problems for the idea that reading a screenplay allows one to see the movie
in her mind’s eye: First, the reader of a screenplay neither sees nor even

212 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
visualizes the movie in her mind’s eye because there is no finished movie
to see or visualize. The screenplay underdetermines or leaves unspecified
many of a movie’s constitutive properties. Now some theorists of a broadly
constructivist persuasion might suggest that the requisite details are filled in
and the work completed by the reader’s visualizations. For example, Steven
Maras invokes the work of Wolfgang Iser and Roman Ingarden to explore
the idea that the reader’s mental visualization of the screenplay may con-
stitute a kind of work-completion (although Ingarden himself would not
have put it that way).*® And a similar thought runs through Elaine Scarry’s
Dreaming by the Book, one of only a handful of major works on visualiza-
tion in the literary arts. Scarry suggests, “a poem or novel is a set of instruc-
tions for mental composition—in something of the same way that a musical
score provides a set of instructions for how to reconstruct the music the
composer heard long ago in his own head.”*
Now I think this line of argument has difficulties that I will not canvass
here, but it is important to see that even if one accepts it, the work com-
pleted through the act of visualization or imaging would be the screenplay
rather than the film.*° It remains the case that no cinematic work is com-
pleted in virtue of one’s visualization for the straightforward reasons that
no cinematic work exists to be completed and one does not create a movie
simply by visualizing one. At best, we can say that someone who is particu-
larly adept at visualizing may be able to conjure especially vivid imagery of
what a cinematic work made from the screenplay could be like.
Clearly, though, a great many readers might be able to visualize in great
detail what a cinematic work made from the screenplay could be like, and
these visualizations may differ radically because the screenplay is silent about
many of the constitutive properties that a film made from it will have. So,
here is the other way in which the nature of the screenplay makes talk about
viewing a movie in the mind’s eye untenable: Even if a reader’s imaging were
extraordinarily detailed and she could visualize an entire “quasimovie,” it
would not be the same quasimovie that any other reader visualized. Pasolini’s
oft-cited reflections on the nature of the screenplay are thus wrong on another
count: There is no “film to which the screenplay the screenplay alludes” that
the reader can “reconstruct in his own head.” For the screenplay does not
allude to any particular film whatsoever and the different readers will of ne-
cessity visualize (“reconstruct” gets the order of events backwards) different
films. It makes no sense to talk about different readers visualizing the movie
or even a movie when reading a screenplay. On the contrary, a screenplay af-
fords the visualization of a number of different potential movies.
This fact may explain the results of a study by lan W. Macdonald, pub-
lished as “Finding the Needle: How Readers See Screen Ideas,” in which
“83% of readers often or always find the screen idea in front of them unat-
tractive.”°' Now one can reasonably assume that screenwriters do not have
similarly negative impressions of the potential films they visualize while
writing. So, what explains the disconnect between what the writers visu-
alize and what the readers visualize? One explanation could be that the

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 213
writers, as a whole, are just not very good at what they do. Perhaps if their
writing were more precise, the readers’ visualizations would more closely
coincide with their own. However, I think a more plausible account is one
that Macdonald himself offers, “This [frequent negative reaction on the part
of readers] in itself is not a condemnation of the quality of the screen ideas
or screenplays; it reflects both the status of these documents as ‘hints fixed
on paper’ (Sergio [sic] Eisenstein, quoted by Mehring 1990:6).”*? But I think
this point needs further emphasis. If, as I have argued, the screenplay does
not specify any particular cinematic work and, further, does not afford its
reader a visualization of any particular cinematic work, then a plausible ex-
planation for Macdonald’s findings is that different readers “see” different
screen ideas in the same screenplay and, indeed, do not visualize very much
(if any) of same imagery conjured by the writer during the writing process.
On the contrary, the screenplay offers readers mere “hints,” as Eisenstein
put it, of what a film made from it could be like. And these “hints” allow
for a great deal of variability in our visualizations.
In summary, I think we have good reasons to dispense with talk of
screenplay-reading as facilitating the viewing of movies in the mind’s eye—
that is, at least in scholarly contexts in which we should be striving for con-
ceptual clarity. This is not to deny that many people do visualize potential
movies while reading a screenplay. I want to maintain that, as Ryle puts it,
“the concept of picturing, visualizing or ‘seeing’ is a proper and useful con-
cept.”*? Moreover, it may be true that in many cases reading a screenplay in-
volves conjuring visual mental imagery of cinematographic representations,
even though this is substantively different from actually perceiving a movie.
Commonly encountered phrases such as “The camera shows us” and
“We see” have a sort of dual function. On the one hand, they are instruc-
tions for what visual images should be captured by the camera. On the
other, they are instructions for conjuring mental imagery—for visualizing
what a given moment, shot, or scene might look like when the film is fin-
ished. This overlap creates a cue for the reader to conjure an image not
just of a character, locale, or event, but of that character, locale, or event as
represented cinematically. Consider the opening of Spike Lee’s screenplay
for She’s Gotta Have It:
EXT. Williamsburg Bridge—Morning
We are in BLACK AND WHITE. Somewhat like the black and
white photography of the 1940s film noir movies. It is a bright
Sunday morning in the Williamsburg section of Brooklyn, New York.
We pan slowly from the bridge to an old warehouse.
This passage uses several means to encourage us to conjure a visual mental
image of the Williamsburg Bridge as represented on film—the indication
of black and white stock, the explicit comparison to 1940s noirs, and the
phrase “we pan.” This, I think, is the kernel of truth in claims about viewing

214 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
movies in the mind’s eye: When we read passages like this, many of us do in
fact conjure an image of a cinematic representation. It may even be the case
that most of us usually conjure such “twofold” imagery (imagery of fictional
events “seen” through an image of a film) whenever we read screenplays.
Indeed, it is often the screenwriter’s objective to have us visualize how the
fictional events she describes might be represented cinematically—put more
prosaically, how she wants the finished film to look. But we must not con-
fuse the plausible possibility of having a visual mental image of a cinematic
representation with the implausible idea that when reading a screenplay
one’s mental imagery is itself in some sense movielike.
Although this capacity to afford a sort of twofolded mental imagery—
imagery of filmic images—may be particular to screenplays among literary
works, it is not the case that the screenplays necessarily cue us to conjure
imagery in this mode. Sometimes, screenwriters cue us to visualize images
that are not intended to be realized in the production process—as when Er-
nest Lehman writes in his final version of the screenplay for Sweet Smell of
Success (1957), “it is the hour when bright dreams have turned to ashes.”°*°
In such cases, the screenplay does not cue us to conjure an image of a cine-
matographic representation (i.e., the ashes captured on film), but simply the
image itself (the ashes). A similar example occurs in Richard Price’s shooting
script for The Color of Money (1986), when the reader is told: “[Eddie]’s
preparing a tight rack of nine-balls by tapping each ball in the diamond
with the cue ball. (Makes them hug each other for maximum dispersion
on the break.) It’s like a priest preparing for Mass.”°° In this instance, the
reader may, arguably, be cued to conjure Eddie’s preparations as represented
cinematically, but she is also cued to conjure an image of a priest, who is
outside of the imaged cinematic representation.
Perhaps the best evidence that screenplays do not necessarily cue readers
to image cinematic representations comes from instances in which we are
cued to conjure imagery that cannot be represented cinematically at all. For
example, some screenplays cue us to conjure olfactory or haptic imagery.
Consider this excerpt from Paul Schrader’s screenplay for Taxi Driver (1976):
It is 3:30 IN THE MORNING in a bacon-shaped all night WEST SIDE
RESTAURANT. The thick smell hangs in the air—fried grease, smoke,
sweat, regurgitated wine.*”
This passage allows many of us, I suspect, to conjure a vivid olfactory image
of the stench in the diner. But in this case, we conjure an olfactory image
of a fictional diner directly, as it were, rather than first imaging a shot of a
diner. Or consider this passage from the pilot script for Dexter (Showtime,
2006-):
And then ina flash, Dexter pulls a syringe out of his pocket, PLUNGES
the needle deep into Donovan’s neck.°*

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 215
Here, too, I think many readers experience vivid mental imagery; in this
case, one might even slightly flinch as one conjures a haptic image of a
needle being plunged deep into a neck. But again, we can conjure this image
without having to first conjure a visual image of a cinematic representation
of the events described. So, there are at least some ways in which, when we
read screenplays, we do not conjure images of cinematic representations or
images of things as if we were seeing them on film.
And although I have offered only a couple of examples to show that con-
juring visual mental imagery of a cinematic representation is not a necessary
part of our screenplay reading experience, I actually think that our conjur-
ing of this sort of twofolded imagery happens quite rarely. That is, when we
conjure images of the fictional events described by a screenplay, rarely do we
“see” those events as mediated by a film that we have also imaged. Rather,
we just image the events themselves. In any case, though, I submit there is
no compelling reason to think that our screenplay-cued mental imagery is
different in kind from mental imagery fostered by more familiar sorts of
literary works, such as poems and novels.
In an illuminating recent essay that builds upon work by Ellen Esrock
and Elaine Scarry, G. Gabrielle Starr outlines various kinds of mental im-
agery that literature can engender.°’ In addition to visual mental imagery,
to which critics and theorists normally devote the most attention, Starr dis-
cusses auditory imagery, motor imagery, olfactory and gustatory imagery,
tactile imagery (or haptic imagery), and multisensory imagery. Starr nicely
illustrates her theoretical discussion with illuminating examples from vari-
ous poems. Significantly, examples of screenplays that cue all of these sorts
of imagery are also readily available. It seems likely that screenplays gener-
ally foster visual mental imagery far more than any other sensory imagery,
but, as we have seen, they also have the ability to engender other sorts of
imagery as well. In addition, it may be true that poetry generally does a
better job of fostering mental imagery—not only of nonvisual imagery, but
perhaps of visual imagery, too. But there are certainly exceptions to the gen-
eralization—screenplays that excel at cuing vivid mental imagery and poems
that cue little imagery (if any) at all.
The fact that screenplays seem able to cue the same sorts of mental im-
agery as do more familiar literary works (and by the same means as those
more familiar works) is yet another reason to suspect that many screenplays
are literature. But, as I have tried to emphasize in this chapter, it is not
just that screenplays themselves resemble literary works in their form or
function. Rather, my point is that screenplays give rise to one of the same
kinds of experiences as works such as poems and novels—one of the experi-
ences that seems central to the appreciation of literature. In other words,
as with other forms of literature, it is a convention of screenplay writ-
ing and screenplay reading—a tacit agreement on the part of writers and
readers—that part of the proper appreciation of the screenplay characteris-
tically involves mental imaging.

216 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
Yet despite these similarities, we also need to bear in mind differences:
When appreciating the ways in which a screenplay cues us to conjure mental
imagery, one still must be attuned to contextual factors ranging from the rel-
atively general to the specific. At a more general level, for example, we must
be sensitive to the convention within Hollywood screenwriting practice that
the screenwriter should only write what will be visible and audible to the
viewer of the finished film—should only attempt to cue visual and auditory
imagery. A more specific contextual factor bearing on the appreciation of
the screenplay could be a writer’s intentions that a particular screenplay be
used for production or not. This may affect the detail with which the writer
attempts to guide our imagery, or the extent to which the writer attempts to
cue imagery in a novel and interesting way or uses shortcuts like clichés. In
summary, a proper appreciation of a screenplay as a literary work involves
reading it with an understanding of how its writer(s) intended it be ap-
proached or used as well as the institutional (broadly understood) conven-
tions within which the writer(s) worked.
CONCLUSION
Although arguing that screenplays may be artworks has not been my sole
task in this book, I have in this chapter, as in others, spent a good deal of
time reflecting on screenplays as literature. Therefore, I would like to close
with a general methodological reflection about resistance to the notion that
the screenplay may be complete work of art in its own right. The skepticism
about this notion comes, I suspect, primarily from those who think that it
does not recognize the complexity of the screenplay and its relationship to
the creation of a film. Steven Maras, for example, writes:
Why should a drift towards an autonomous activity of screenwriting
be of concern? Because it changes what it means to make film or
cinema ... [T]he movement towards autonomy takes the script out of
its production context and potentially reinforces a fracture between
conception and execution that impacts on the way we might imagine
creativity and expression, and think about the medium.
The general worry here seems to be that thinking about the screenplay as
an autonomous work closes down space for dissident screenwriting prac-
tices by normalizing one particular mode of screenwriting at the expense of
others. Maras already regards the screenplay as an idea around which “a
normative idea of screenwriting . . . has been reinvented,”*! and I suppose
his concern is that granting the autonomy of the screenplay would further
abet the shaping of a normative idea of screenwriting.
The motivations underlying such worries are laudable, but I believe
the concern is unfounded for several reasons. First, amy artistic, or more

The Appreciation of the Screenplay as Literature 217
broadly, cultural, practice whose boundaries are established by the conven-
tions and standards of the practice is normative in some sense. One cannot
write an opera without any words because the conventions of the practice
have in this sense made the idea of an opera a normative one: operas, as we
understand them, must involve singing. In the same way, the screenplay is
a normative concept insofar as the conventions established by our practices
have come to require that screenplays also have words.”
However, there is nothing insidious about our concept of the screenplay
being normative in this way. Obviously, one can imagine certain artistic or
other cultural practices being normative in infelicitous ways—but it hardly
follows from this that all normative concepts are necessarily so. The con-
cept of a film, for example, is innocuously normative insofar as conventions
require that there be an image on the negative that we project onto the
screen. The fact that the concept is normative in this sense does not foreclose
upon the artistic possibilities of filmmakers. For example, Walter Ruttmann
challenged this convention by making a film, Weekend (1930), which had
sounds but no image—just a black screen. This experiment likely spurred
both viewers and other practitioners to rethink the boundaries of movie-
hood. In short, all artistic practices are necessarily normative insofar as they
establish standards for something falling under the practice. Second, there
is no reason to think that acknowledging that some screenplays are works
of art in their own right would privilege a particular mode of screenwriting
practice anyway.
Finally, the worry about normalization is stated in oddly normative
terms. Maras expresses a rather general concern that thinking about screen-
plays as autonomous works could change what it means to make film. I fail
to see either why this should be regarded as a problem or why we should be
committed to trying to stabilize what it means to make film. Is this not just
normalizing the screenplay in a different way? Maras also worries about the
idea of the screenplay being removed from its production context. But there
is no reason to think that reading a screenplay as an autonomous work pre-
cludes us from studying how the same screenplay is developed in a produc-
tion context. Reading Bertolt Brecht’s Life of Galileo as a work of literature
in no way forecloses upon the possibilities of reading and studying the play
as it was interwoven with the production of a work for the stage. Again, the
suggestion that we should not treat the screenplay in a certain way is what
seems to be the normative force here.
In sum, if the goal of theorizing the screenplay is really to explain what
the screenplay is and what kind of a thing it is according to our practices,
then our theories must be genuinely constrained by those practices, even if
this results in a conception of the screenplay that is not to our taste—for
example, if recognizing the literary status of some screenplays feeds into
the capitalist designs of the publishing industry. Furthermore, if, as I have
argued, our practices determine the boundaries of our screenplay concept,
and that concept is, thus, an historical one, we must constantly revise our

218 A Philosophy of the Screenplay
theories about the screenplay as our practices change. This will require work
and flexibility—especially if a perceived “drift” toward a particular mode
of practice appears somehow problematic or to diverge with our previously
existing theories. But this is the price of good theory.
Of course, if this general methodological argument is accepted, and
I am right about the primacy of practice and the essential historicity of the
screenplay, then I ought to stand ready to modify the more specific ideas
about the screenplay that I have proposed. I am happy to do so, and, indeed,
hope that as work on the screenplay progresses, others will track and an-
alyze emerging screenplay writing and screenplay reading practices in ways
that prompt them to refine and constructively revise the ideas I have offered
here.

(perhaps even in different departments) create diagrams and sketches, and
various other production team members evaluate and use such diagrams and
sketches as if they were distinct from the screenplays with which they are
associated. Furthermore, outside of production contexts, I think a critical ap-
preciation or evaluation of a screenplay such as Beckett’s “Film” treats the
screenplay as a verbal object; such an appreciation or evaluation does not
involve attending to Beckett’s skill (or lack thereof) at creating diagrams.

. Here I wish to avoid becoming bogged down in philosophical debates about

eral account of the nature of artifacts I sketched earlier. The fan-fiction author
has a “substantive and substantively correct” conception of a screenplay and
has succeeded in “making one of those things.” In the account I have offered,
she cannot fail to make a screenplay in these circumstances, and the burden
of proof is on the skeptic to show that she has failed to do so. I am grateful to
Noél Carroll for raising this objection.

CHAPTER 3
Ls One might wonder why I do not claim that all screenplays are artworks if
screenwriting is an art practice. My reasoning here stems from thinking about
other art practices (or art forms): Although filmmaking is an art practice,
not all films are works of art. So, too, I think it is with screenwriting and
screenplays.

. Perhaps it is worth reiterating here how the historical narrative approach
deals with extremely abrupt, revolutionary innovations in an artworld. The
approach supposes that no matter how revolutionary a work or practice is,
such a work or practice cannot be a complete break with the past if it is actu-
ally art. For if it were truly a complete break, then we would have no reason
to think that it was art. Assuming, however, an extremely revolutionary work
does have some connection to prior works and practices, then the problem of
elucidating that connection is simply an epistemological problem—not one
that threatens the intentional-historical theory itself. See Carroll, “Art, Prac-
tice, and Narrative,” 70.

. Some while after this chapter was written, shortly before publication, I dis-
covered Steven Price’s helpful article, “The First Screenplays? American Mu-
toscope and Biograph Scenarios Revisited,” Journal of Screenwriting 2, no. 2
(2011), 195-213. Price provides a much more detailed consideration of
Loughney’s discovery than I offer here. Like me, he is interested in the ques-
tion of why the AM&B scenarios so closely resemble contemporaneous the-
atrical scripts. He seems to find plausible the explanation for which I argue
here—generally, that writing for the stage and for the screen were conceived
as the same thing; or, at least that the scenarios were “dual-medium”—but
also rightly points out that other possible interpretations of the evidence are at
hand. Where we differ, if I understand him correctly, is that while he remains
uncommitted to a particular explanation, I think the account I sketch is the
most plausible one on offer. However, I believe our general assessments of the
evidence are largely similar, and I agree with the gist of his conclusion, “The
coincidence of the timing of these copyright disputes and the depositing of
the AM&B scenarios suggests that the legal debate about what constituted a
dramatic composition brought into being, both conceptually and formally, the
very idea of what we would now term the ‘screenplay’ ” (199).

“Photoplay” refers here to a movie, but the word was also sometimes used to
refer to a script. The term was supposedly the winning entry in Essanay’s 1910
competition to create a more complimentary name for movies. If this is true,
the term must have found widespread acceptance quite quickly; Photoplay
magazine began just a year later, in 1911. In any case, the industry’s adoption
of the word seems symptomatic of two facts: First, we see again that cinema
(or at least the story film) was conceived of as a dramatic art. Second, it is clear
that this conception was promulgated by the industry, which had a vested
interest in doing so. For, calling movies “photoplays” undoubtedly helped the
manufacturers in their efforts to establish cinema as a quality product. Both
“photoplay” and its other formulations, such as “photoplaywright,” clearly
tie cinema and scenarios to the theater in the same manner as early terms such
as “moving picture play” and “silent drama.” Evidence shows that the term
remained in currency at least well into the 1930s, and Photoplay magazine
continued to publish under the same title until 1980. For a discussion, see
Maras, 132-137.

ja As Staiger puts it, “The fact that in later years these scripts became known as
‘continuities’ is traceable to the emphasis Hollywood filmmakers placed on
the script’s ability to prearrange the conventional cues to temporal and spatial
relations.” The term “continuity” seems to have shifted from referring to the
quality of spatio-temporal continuity to referring to the shot-by-shot outline
that ensured that quality. In 1916, Epes Winthrop Sargent defines continuity
as “the uninterrupted action of a story or the arrangement of a plot to avoid
or explain interruptions of time or plot,” but by 1919, Esenwein and Leeds
use the term, “continuity,” synonymously with scenario form or outline form
that is meant to achieve spatio-temporal continuity. See Staiger, “Blueprints,”
180; Epes Winthrop Sargent, Technique of the Photoplay, 3rd Edition (New
York: Chalmers Publishing Company, 1916), 360; Esenwein and Leeds, Writ-
ing Revised Ed., 19, 26.

by scenes, the dramatic scene-sequence of the proposed story from which the

regarding theatrical scripts below, and elaborate upon these remarks in the

. See Paisley Livingston, Art and Intention (Oxford: Oxford University Press,
2007), 53-61. I realize that many scholars working in literary studies and film
studies are suspicious of intentions. Steven Price, for example, writes: “ ‘Inten-
tion’ in literary studies seems to presuppose an individual subject that does
the intending; it is therefore bound up with Romantic notions of authorship”
(The Screenplay, 48). Replying in full to this sort of objection would require
another book, so I will simply direct readers to the one Livingston has already
written. All I will say here is that, following Livingston’s work, I have in mind
a much more nuanced notion of intention, which is certainly compatible with
concepts like collaborative authorship. But I also fail to see the putatively
necessary link between “an individual subject that does the intending” and
“Romantic conceptions of authorship,” which, for Price, involves “the myth
of solitary genius.” Surely we can plausibly posit that my hypothetical anima-
tion screenplay is an outcome of my successfully realized intentions (that is,
those successfully realized intentions of an individual subject). How is this
reasonable claim supposed to involve “Romantic conceptions of authorship,”
let alone “the myth of solitary genius?”

ics and Art Criticism 66, no. 1 (Winter 2008), 74.
at Livingston, Art and Intention, 58. Livingston refers to the completion of

completion.” Also see Livingston’s reply to Hick, “When a Work is Finished:

60. Pace Price, this is not to suggest that a screenplay formed piecemeal, by mul-
tiple writers, is created entirely in the absence of intentionality—in his words,
that “in the case of the screenplay [. . .] it may be inappropriate even to
speak of intentions at all” (The Screenplay, 99). How a screenplay could be
created entirely without intentions is unclear to me. Following Livingston,
I think a more nuanced concept of intentionality will reveal that each of
the screenplay’s multiple writers must have acted on at least some proximal
intentions—to put pen to paper, to craft a coherent narrative, and so forth.
As Livingston puts it in Art and Intention, “when a writer puts words on

the page, an executive attitude—a settledness on writing now—is adopted in

. The more difficult and interesting question about unfinished works is not
whether they are art, but rather, as Peter Lamarque suggests, whether such
works constitute a distinct category of artworks that “invite their own special
modes of appreciation, related to, but not identical with, the modes associated
with completed works.” Surely there are some screenplays—perhaps many—
that are not genetically complete; this specific class of screenplays we may
properly call unfinished and it seems to me that although this classification
itself should not cast doubt on their status as literary works, it may very well
require a different sort of appreciation from that with which we read works
that are genetically complete. See Peter Lamarque, Work and Object (Oxford:
Oxford University Press, 2010), 36-37.

eke Gregory Currie, An Ontology of Art (London: Palgrave Macmillan, 1989);

te Davies, Art as Performance, 16-24. There has been a good deal of debate
about how Davies conceives of the pragmatic constraint—particularly be-
cause he goes on to offer an ontology of art that strikes some philosophers as
failing to line up with our practices. Robert Stecker parses the matter quite
helpfully, explaining that the debate turns on which practices one takes to be
relevant. Stecker points out that for Davies, it is our “reflective critical and
appreciative practices” (377) that must constrain our ontological theoriz-
ing. I do not have space to discuss it here, but Davies’s pragmatic constraint
seems to me too weak because “reflective” opens the door for highly revision-
ist theories that do not line up with our ordinary creative and appreciative
practices. I lean closer to the other camp Stecker describes—that which takes
our referential practices (what we say about works in our ordinary discourse)
as constraining our ontological theorizing. However, in what follows, I em-
phasize the centrality of our ordinary creative and appreciative practices and
intuitions rooted in them over our ordinary speech. Thus, I believe I am im-
mune to the objections Stecker raises against “the semantic constraint.” See
Robert Stecker, “Methodological Questions about the Ontology of Music,”
Journal of Aesthetics and Art Criticism 67, no. 4 (Fall 2009), 375-386, esp.
376-378.

. Ibid., 221. Very shortly before this book went to publication, I encountered
Julian Dodd’s article, “Defending the Discovery Model in the Ontology of
Art: A Reply to Amie Thomasson on the Qua Problem,” British Journal of
Aesthetics 52, no. 1 (January 2012), 75-95. Basically, Dodd’s argument is
that there is a different solution to the qua problem from that which Thomas-
son proposes—one that does not necessitate abandoning the discovery view.
Dodd’s challenge, of course, threatens my arguments here, which follow
Thomasson’s work extremely closely. Unfortunately, due to time constraints,

Notes 245
I will have to leave consideration of his article for another occasion. But per-
haps there is an argument to be made that the qua problem aside, insofar as
artifacts remain ontologically dependent upon human intentions, there are
still plausibly ontological facts about them that are indeterminate and not
open to empirical discovery. It just is not clear to me that there is something
we could discover about the nature of, say, architecture, independent of our
practices, that established how much of an architectural work could be de-
molished or replaced before the work was lost. Indeed, there is an argument
to be made that the answer to this question will be different across differ-
ent cultures with different architectural practices. See, for example, Lopes,
“Shikinen Sengu and the Ontology of Architecture in Japan,” 77-84.
41. However, as Thomasson writes, “although such massive mistakes about the
ontology of art are not possible, the limits of epistemic privilege must be
carefully acknowledged: the relevant epistemic privilege is only collective,
not individual, and concerns only ontological features of the kind referred to.
The above alone does not entail that there is any protection from error about,
for example, the causal role of the works of art in the relevant culture, any
functions they can or do serve, their histories of production, aesthetic proper-
ties, and so forth. All grounders are assured of (collectively) is that, if there
is any art-kind referred to by the terms they attempt to ground the reference
of, it has the ontological standing they commonly (if tacitly) understand and
treat it as having” (Thomasson, “Ontology of Art and Knowledge,” 227).

aa I doubt it is controversial to claim that the contents of percepts are not in-
tentionally determined; we may choose an object at which to look, but our
percepts exist independently of our intentions and beliefs. On the other hand,
the former claim—that the contents of mental images can be intentionally
determined—requires a few brief qualifications. First, the contents of our
mental images are not necessarily entirely determined by our intentions. One
may have a visual mental image that bears a causal relationship to a percept
in addition to an agent’s mental action—as when I conjure an image of my
son’s face the first time I held him. Second, one may involuntarily conjure an
image—as when one has an unwelcome visual mental image of a snake or

Notes 249
some other unpleasant critter. Third, dreams—plausibly a variety of mental
imagery—may be entirely outside one’s conscious control. All of these points
are accurate, but do not tell against the main claim I am making here. Dreams
aside (for they seem to constitute a special kind of mental imagery), one
determines—at least partly intentionally, in most cases—the contents of the
images one conjures. Indeed, even when one has an image involuntarily, one
still has the ability to manipulate the image. In short, imaging is something we
do and images are things we ourselves create.

37, Again, I am setting aside dreams, which plausibly may surprise us, as a sepa-
rate sort of imagery. Nevertheless, it is worth noting that even though it might
be possible for us to be surprised by our dreams, one sometimes does, in the
middle of a dream, know what is going to happen next. Even though this may
happen rarely in dreams, it is an impossibility in the context of perceiving a
movie or reading a screenplay one has never encountered before.

ries. See, for recent examples, Zenon W. Pylyshyn, “Mental Imagery: In Search

against it. Reviewing them all here is beyond my purview.
